Schauder estimates for parabolic p p -Laplace systems
Verena Bögelein, Frank Duzaar, Ugo Gianazza, Naian Liao, Christoph Scheven

Abstract
. We establish the local Hölder regularity of the spatial gradient of bounded weak solutions u : E T → ℝ k u\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{k} to the non-linear system of parabolic type ∂ t u − div ⁡ ( a ⁡ ( x , t ) ​ ( μ 2 + | D ​ u | 2 ) p − 2 2 ​ D ​ u ) = 0 in E T , \partial_{t}u-\Div\Big(a(x,t)\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}Du\Big)=0\qquad\mbox{in $E_{T}$}, where p > 1 p>1 , μ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \mu\in[0,1] , and the coefficient a ∈ L ∞ ​ ( E T ) a\in L^{\infty}(E_{T}) is bounded below by a positive constant and is Hölder continuous in the space variable x x . As an application, we prove Hölder estimates for the gradient of weak solutions to a doubly non-linear parabolic equation in the super-critical fast diffusion regime.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35K65, 35K67, 35B45, 35B65, 35K92, 76S05

1.
Introduction

Let
E
be a bounded open set in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
E_{T}:=E\times(0,T]
for some
T>0
. Consider weak solutions
u\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
to the following non-linear system of parabolic type

(1.1)

\partial_{t}u-\Div\Big(a(x,t)\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}Du\Big)=0\qquad\mbox{in $E_{T}$},

for parameters
p>1
and
\mu\in[0,1]
, where the coefficient
a\in L^{\infty}(E_{T})
satisfies

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}C_{o}\leq a(x,t)\leq C_{1},\\[6.0pt]
|a(x,t)-a(y,t)|\leq C_{1}|x-y|^{\alpha},\end{array}\right.

for a.e.
x,y\in E
and
t\in(0,T]
, with a Hölder exponent

\alpha\in(0,1)
and structural constants
0<C_{o}\leq C_{1}
.
For
bounded
weak solutions to this system, we establish local Hölder regularity of their spatial gradient.
The precise result, including the corresponding quantitative estimates of Schauder-type, is stated as follows. Notation can be found in Section
2.1
.

Theorem 1.1

(Schauder estimate for parabolic
p
-Laplace systems)
.

Let
p>1
and
\mu\in[0,1]
. Then there exist a constant

C>0
and a Hölder exponent
\alpha_{o}\in(0,1)
both depending on
N,p,C_{o},C_{1},\alpha
and
k
such that, whenever
u
is a bounded weak solution to the parabolic system
(
1.1
) with (
1.2
), we have

Du\in C^{\alpha_{o},\alpha_{o}/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{kN}\big).

Moreover, for any compact subset
\mathsf{K}\subset E_{T}

with
\varrho:=\tfrac{1}{4}\min\{1,\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{par}}(\mathsf{K},\partial_{\mathrm{par}}E_{T})\}
, we have
the quantitative local
L^{\infty}
-gradient estimate

(1.3)

\sup_{\mathsf{K}}|Du|\leq C\bigg[\frac{\operatornamewithlimits{osc}_{E_{T}}u}{\varrho}+\Big(\frac{\operatornamewithlimits{osc}_{E_{T}}u}{\varrho}\Big)^{\frac{2}{p}}+\mu\bigg]=:C\lambda,

and the quantitative local gradient Hölder estimate

(1.4)

|Du(x_{1},t_{1})-Du(x_{2},t_{2})|\leq C\lambda\Bigg[\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|+\sqrt{\lambda^{p-2}|t_{1}-t_{2}|}}{\min\{1,\lambda^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\}\varrho}\Bigg]^{\alpha_{o}}

for any
(x_{1},t_{1}),(x_{2},t_{2})\in\mathsf{K}
.

In their groundbreaking works
[
18
,
19
,
20
]
DiBenedetto & Friedman established Hölder regularity of the gradient of solutions to the model
p
-Laplace system; they covered the super-critical range
p>p_{\ast}:=\frac{2N}{N+2}
. For an extension to the sub-critical range
1<p\leq p_{*}
see
[
15
]
, although for computational simplicity only the scalar case (
k=1
) was considered. These works dealt with model cases without coefficients,
whereas systems of parabolic
p
-Laplace-type with Hölder continuous coefficients were treated in

[
14
,
32
,
36
]
, focusing on
p>p_{*}
. Furthermore, the case of variable exponents
p(x,t)>p_{\ast}
was included in
[
6
]
.

The main new feature of our result is
that we cover systems with Hölder continuous coefficients for arbitrary exponents
p>1
. Moreover, Schauder-type estimates are given in terms of the oscillation of the solution; we will show an interesting application of such novel explicit estimates in Section
6
. Below the critical exponent
p_{\ast}
, weak solutions need not be locally bounded; see the counterexamples in
[
8
]
. Therefore, Schauder-type estimates cannot
be expected to hold for arbitrary weak solutions
to (
1.1
) if

1<p\leq p_{*}
. However, we are able to establish a unified theory for
bounded

solutions. In turn, local boundedness of solutions can be established under the assumption
u\in L^{r}_{\loc}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
for some
r>\frac{N(2-p)}{p}
. Therefore, the local boundedness assumption could in fact be replaced by this integrability assumption.

Part of this manuscript has been extracted and extended from the arXiv preprint
[
8
]
which does not deal with systems.

1.1.
Method of proof

The proof of the theorem is divided into two steps. As a first step, in Section
3
, we consider systems with differentiable coefficients. In order to avoid further technical complication that arises from the degeneracy of the system, in such a case we restrict ourselves to the case
\mu>0
. However, it is crucial that all constants are independent of
\mu
. The second step in Section
5
deals with systems with Hölder continuous coefficients. The method consists in considering a comparison problem with frozen coefficients: Relevant comparison estimates are prepared in Section
4
.

The most important results in Section
3
are the quantitative gradient bounds of solutions. To get them, we adapt a general scheme in
[
17
, Chapter VIII, Section 4]
, that is, we will first reach
|Du|\in L^{\infty}_{\loc}(E_{T})
in a qualitative way; then, we will turn such qualitative information into precise quantitative estimates.
Special care is needed for the sub-critical range
1<p\leq\frac{2N}{N+2}
as solutions might be unbounded.
To get proper gradient bounds, we need to work with
bounded
solutions, adapt a trick from
[
17
, Chapter VIII, Lemma 4.1]
(see also
[
20
,
15
]
), and raise the integrability of
|Du|
by a clever manipulation of the energy estimate for second order derivatives in (
3.2
).
This is a unified treatment for all exponents
1<p\leq 2
among
bounded
solutions and yields an estimate of the
L^{m}
-norm of
|Du|
for every
m>1
in terms of its
L^{p}
-norm and the oscillation of
u
, cf. Lemma
3.3
. With this result at hand, we run Moser’s and De Giorgi’s iterations to obtain the gradient bound in terms of the
L^{p}
-norm of
|Du|
and the oscillation of
u
.

Once the boundedness of the gradient is established
in the case of differentiable coefficients, then the Hölder continuity of the gradient follows for all exponents
p>1
from the theory developed in the seminal works by DiBenedetto & Friedman
[
18
,
19
,
20
]
, see also
[
13
,
15
]

and the monograph
[
17
, Chapter IX]
.

The aim of Section
5
is to generalize these regularity results to systems with merely Hölder continuous coefficients. The strategy for this is to compare the given solution with a solution to a related problem with frozen coefficients.
This is also the underlying idea in the earlier work
[
6
]
, which covers variable exponents
p(x,t)>\frac{2N}{N+2}
. However, in the present work we develop
a slightly different approach than that in

[
6
]
, which allows us to include the sub-critical
case, and meanwhile, contains some simplifications also in the super-critical case. First we prove
a priori
estimates under the additional assumption that the gradient is bounded.
The virtual advantage of this approach is that we are able to work with cylinders
Q_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}
of a fixed geometry given
by the parameter
\lambda
, which can be chosen in terms of
\|Du\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
This represents a significant simplification compared to
[
6
]
, in which the authors were forced to use a sequence of cylinders
Q_{\varrho_{i}}^{(\lambda_{i})}
with varying parameters
\lambda_{i}
that take into account a possible blow-up of the gradient.
In order to work with cylinders of a fixed geometry, it is crucial that the article
[
11
]
provides an improved version of a Campanato-type estimate for differentiable coefficients that holds on cylinders
Q_{r}^{(\lambda)}
with a fixed geometry given by
\lambda>0
and arbitrary
r\in(0,\varrho]
, cf. Proposition
5.1
. In Section
5
, we prove that this Campanato-type estimate
can be extended to systems with merely Hölder continuous coefficients, assuming that the gradient is bounded. This yields the desired
a priori
estimates. Finally, using these
a priori
estimates we prove Theorem
1.1
by an approximation argument.

We note that in the super-critical case
p>\frac{2N}{N+2}
, the method described above can also be used to derive gradient bounds that are independent of the oscillation of the solution and depend only on integrals of
|Du|^{p}
instead. The necessary changes are indicated in Remark
5.5
. Consequently, in the super-critical range, our approach is not limited to the treatment of bounded solutions.

2.
Preliminaries and notation

2.1.
Notation

Throughout the manuscript
\mathbb{N}_{0}
denotes
\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, while
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbb{R}^{N}
are Euclidean spaces. Define
E_{T}:=E\times(0,T]
with
E
a bounded open set of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
T>0
.
Given a
scalar
function
v\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
, by
\nabla v
we always mean its spatial gradient, whereas for a
vector valued
function
u\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, its spatial gradient will be denoted by
Du
.

Moreover, we have

a\vee b:=\max\{a,b\}.

For a point
z_{o}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we always write
z_{o}=(x_{o},t_{o})
.
By
B_{\varrho}(x_{o})

we denote the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with center
x_{o}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and radius
\varrho>0
. When
x_{o}=0
we simply write
B_{\varrho}
, omitting the reference to the center.

We use the symbol

Q_{R,S}(z_{o}):=B_{R}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-S,t_{o}]

to denote a
general backward parabolic cylinder
with the indicated parameters. Occasionally, we also use
\sigma Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\equiv Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}(z_{o})
for
\sigma>0
.
For
\lambda>0
we define a second type of
backward intrinsic parabolic cylinders

(2.1)

Q_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(z_{o}):=B_{\varrho}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-\lambda^{2-p}\varrho^{2},t_{o}].

If
\lambda=1
, then we omit it. When
z_{o}=(0,0)
or the context is clear, we also omit
z_{o}
.

The
parabolic boundary
of
E_{T}
is given by the union of its lateral and initial boundary

\partial_{\mathrm{par}}E_{T}:=\big(\overline{E}\times\{0\}\big)\cup\big(\partial E\times(0,T]\big).

For two points
z_{1}=(x_{1},t_{1}),z_{2}=(x_{2},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
their
parabolic distance
is defined as

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}(z_{1},z_{2}):=|x_{1}-x_{2}|+\sqrt{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}.

The associated distance of a subset
Q\subset E_{T}
to the parabolic boundary
\partial_{\mathrm{par}}E_{T}
is

\dist_{\mathrm{par}}(Q,\partial_{\mathrm{par}}E_{T}):=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{1}\in Q\\
z_{2}\in\partial_{\mathrm{par}}E_{T}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}(z_{1},z_{2}).

For
\lambda>0
we also define the following
intrinsic
version of the
parabolic distance

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}^{(\lambda)}(z_{1},z_{2}):=|x_{1}-x_{2}|+\sqrt{\lambda^{p-2}|t_{1}-t_{2}|}.

Although
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}^{(\lambda)}

also depends on
p
, since
p
is always fixed we suppress this dependence.

Given a
p
-summable function
v\colon A\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, with a measurable set
A
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, whenever we want to explicitly point out the set where the function takes values, we write
v\in L^{p}(A,\mathbb{R}^{k})
. In the same way, for example,
v\in C^{\infty}_{0}(E,[0,1])
denotes a function
v\in C^{\infty}_{0}(E)
defined on an open set
E
which takes values in
[0,1]
.

For a function
v\in L^{1}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
, and a measurable subset
A\subset E
with positive
\mathcal{L}^{N}
-measure we define the slice-wise mean
(v)_{A}\colon(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
of
v
on
A
by

(v)_{A}(t):=\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}\!\int_{A}v(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad\mbox{for a.e. $t\in(0,T)$.}

Note that the slice-wise mean is defined for any
t\in(0,T)
if
v\in C(0,T;L^{1}(E))
.
If the set
A
is a ball
B_{\varrho}(x_{o})
, then we write
(v)_{x_{o},\varrho}(t)
for short. Similarly, for a measurable set
Q\subset E_{T}
of positive
\mathcal{L}^{N+1}
-measure we define the mean value
(v)_{Q}
of
v
on
Q
by

(v)_{Q}:=\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q}v\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

If the set
Q
is a parabolic cylinder
Q_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(z_{o})
of the type (
2.1
), we write
(v)_{z_{o},\varrho}^{(\lambda)}
for short.
When it is clear from the context, which vertex
x_{o}
or
(x_{o},t_{o})
is meant, we will omit the vertex from the above symbols for simplicity.

2.2.
Parabolic function spaces

Let
X
be a Banach space with its natural norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
, and
T>0
.
For
1\leq p<\infty
we denote by
L^{p}(0,T;X)
the (parabolic) Bochner space of all (strongly) measurable functions

v\colon[0,T]\to X
such that the Bochner norm is finite, i.e.

\|v\|_{L^{p}(0,T;X)}:=\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\|v(t)\|_{X}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty.

Similarly, for
p=\infty
the Bochner space
L^{\infty}(0,T;X)
consists of all (strongly) measurable functions
v\colon[0,T]\to X
, such that
t\to\|v(t)\|_{X}
is essentially bounded, which means that

\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;X)}:=\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{t\in[0,T]}\|v(t)\|_{X}<\infty.

These spaces are Banach spaces. We could have defined these spaces on
(0,T)
or
(0,T]
, which makes no difference at all, because in the end we get the same spaces. Finally, the Bochner space
C([0,T];X)
denotes the space of all continuous functions
v\colon[0,T]\to X
.
The Bochner norm on this space is given by

\|v\|_{C([0,T];X)}:=\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|v(t)\|_{X}.

Next, the space
C((0,T);X)
consists of all continuous functions
v\colon(0,T)\to X
.
Alternatively,
C([0,T];X)
could be defined as the space of all continuous functions

v\colon(0,T)\to X
that have a continuous extension (still denoted by
v
) to
[0,T]
. Analogous definitions hold for
C((0,T];X)
and
C([0,T);X)
.

Given a bounded, connected, open set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
and the corresponding cylinder
E_{T}
, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
the parabolic Hölder space
C^{\alpha,\alpha/2}(\bar{E}_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
consists of all functions
u\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, which admit continuous extension to
\bar{E}_{T}
, and such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\langle u(\cdot,t)\rangle^{(\alpha)}_{x,E_{T}}+\sup_{x\in\bar{E}}\langle u(x,\cdot)\rangle^{(\alpha/2)}_{t,E_{T}}<\infty.

Here, we used the short-hand abbreviation

\displaystyle\langle u(\cdot,t)\rangle^{(\alpha)}_{x,E_{T}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x,x^{\prime}\in\bar{E}}\frac{|u(x,t)-u(x^{\prime},t)|}{|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha}},

\displaystyle\langle u(x,\cdot)\rangle^{(\alpha)}_{t,E_{T}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{t,t^{\prime}\in[0,T]}\frac{|u(x,t)-u(x,t^{\prime})|}{|t-t^{\prime}|^{\alpha}}.

The space
C^{\alpha,\alpha/2}(\bar{E}_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
is a Banach space. Local variants are defined as usual by letting
C^{\alpha,\alpha/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
the space of functions
v\in C^{\alpha,\alpha/2}(\bar{Q}_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
for any compactly contained sub-cylinder
\bar{Q}_{T}\Subset E_{T}
. The range
\mathbb{R}^{k}
that appears in various function spaces is omitted if
k=1
or the context is clear.

2.3.
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma 2.1
.

[
10
, Lemma 2.3]

Let
A>1
,
\kappa>1
,
C>0
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, we have

\prod_{j=1}^{i}A^{\frac{\kappa^{i-j+1}}{C(\kappa^{i}-1)}}=A^{\frac{\kappa}{C(\kappa-1)}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\prod_{j=1}^{i}A^{\frac{j\kappa^{i-j+1}}{C(\kappa^{i}-1)}}\leq A^{\frac{\kappa^{2}}{C(\kappa-1)^{2}}}.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
24
, Lemma 6.1, p.191]

Let
0<\vartheta<1
,
A,B,C\geq 0
and
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
. Then there exists a universal constant
c=c(\alpha,\beta,\vartheta)

such that, whenever
\phi\colon[R_{o},R_{1}]\times[S_{1},S_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}
is non-negative bounded function satisfying

\phi(r_{1},s_{1})\leq\vartheta\phi(r_{2},s_{2})+\frac{A}{(s_{2}-s_{1})^{\alpha}}+\frac{B}{(r_{2}-r_{1})^{\beta}}+C

for all
R_{o}\leq r_{1}<r_{2}\leq R_{1}
,
S_{1}\leq s_{1}<s_{2}\leq S_{2}
,
we have

\phi(\varrho,\sigma)\leq c\,\bigg[\frac{A}{(s-\sigma)^{\alpha}}+\frac{B}{(r-\varrho)^{\beta}}+C\bigg]

for any
R_{o}\leq\varrho<r\leq R_{1}
and
S_{1}\leq\sigma<s\leq S_{2}
.

For the next lemma we refer to
[
1
]
when
1<p<2
and
[
23
]
when
p\geq 2
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mu\in(0,1]
and
p>1
. For any
\xi,\tilde{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{Nk}
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\mu^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\xi-\big(\mu^{2}+|\tilde{\xi}|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\tilde{\xi}\Big|\leq C\big(\mu^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+|\tilde{\xi}|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\xi-\tilde{\xi}|

and

\displaystyle\Big(\big(\mu^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\xi-\big(\mu^{2}+|\tilde{\xi}|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\tilde{\xi}\Big)\cdot\big(\xi-\tilde{\xi}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{C}\big(\mu^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+|\tilde{\xi}|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\xi-\tilde{\xi}|^{2},

with a positive constant
C=C(p)
.

Weak solutions to parabolic equations or systems in general do not possess a time derivative in the Sobolev sense. On the other hand, it is desirable to use weak solutions in testing functions. In order to overcome this problem, proper mollifications in the time variable are often employed. A simple version, termed the Steklov average, is sufficient for many occasions.
Indeed, given a function
v\in L^{1}(E_{T})
and
0<h<T
, we define its
forward Steklov-average

[v]_{h}
by

[v]_{h}(x,t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\displaystyle{\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}v(x,s)\,\mathrm{d}s,}&t\in(0,T-h),\\[9.0pt]
0,&t\in(T-h,T)\,.\end{array}\right.

Steklov averages are differentiable with respect to
t
. More precisely, we have

\partial_{t}[v]_{h}(x,t)=\tfrac{1}{h}\big(v(x,t+h)-v(x,t)\big)

for almost every
(x,t)\in E\times(0,T-h)
.
The relevant properties of Steklov averages are summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.4
.

For any
r\geq 1
we have

(i)

If
v\in L^{r}(E_{T})
, then
[v]_{h}\in L^{r}(E_{T})
. Moreover,

\|[v]_{h}\|_{L^{r}(E_{T})}\leq\|v\|_{L^{r}(E_{T})}

and

[v]_{h}\to v
in
L^{r}(E_{T})
and almost everywhere on
E_{T}

as
h\downarrow 0
.

(ii)

If
Dv\in L^{r}(E_{T})
, then
D[v]_{h}=[Dv]_{h}\to Du
in
L^{r}(E_{T})
and almost everywhere on
E_{T}
as
h\downarrow 0
.

(iii)

If
v\in C\big(0,T;L^{r}(E)\big)
, then
[v]_{h}(\cdot,t)\to v(\cdot,t)
in
L^{r}(E)

as
h\downarrow 0
for every
t\in(0,T-\varepsilon)
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,T)
.

2.4.
Existence of weak solutions

Let us consider
the following Cauchy-Dirichlet Problem:

(2.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}u-\Div\big(a(x,t)\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}Du\big)=0&\quad\mbox{in $E_{T}$,}\\[6.0pt]
u=g&\quad\mbox{on $\partial E\times(0,T]$,}\\[6.0pt]
u(\cdot,0)=u_{o}&\quad\mbox{in $E$,}\end{array}\right.

where
\mu\in[0,1]
,
(x,t)\mapsto a(x,t)
is a measurable function satisfying
0<C_{o}\leq a\leq C_{1}
,
p>1
,
u_{o}\in L^{2}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
g\in L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k}))
with
\partial_{t}g\in L^{p^{\prime}}(0,T;W^{-1,p^{\prime}}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k}))
.
We first define what we mean by a weak solution to the Cauchy-Dirichlet Problem (
2.2
).

Definition 2.5
.

A function
u

is termed a
weak solution to the Cauchy-Dirichlet Problem (
2.2
)
, if

u\in C\big([0,T];L^{2}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k})\big)\cap g+L^{p}\big(0,T;W_{0}^{1,p}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k})\big)

and if the integral identity

(2.3)

\iint_{E_{T}}\Big[(u_{o}-u)\cdot\partial_{t}\zeta+a(x,t)\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}Du\cdot D\zeta\Big]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=0

is satisfied for any test function

\zeta\in W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k}))\cap L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}_{0}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k}))

such that
\zeta(\cdot,T)=0
.

Note that in the setting of Definition
2.5
, the integral identity (
2.3
) implies that
u(\cdot,0)=u_{o}
in the sense of
L^{2}(E)
; see
[
8
, Proposition 4.9]
for
q=1
.

The issue of the existence of weak solutions is extensively studied in the literature, often under quite general assumptions on the structure of the diffusion part; in our case the existence
result can be deduced, for instance, from
[
40
, Chapter III, Proposition 4.1 and Example 4.A]
. We will need the existence result in Sections
4
and
5
.

A related concept that dispenses with any initial/boundary data is as follows.

Definition 2.6
.

A function
u

is termed a
weak solution
to (
1.1
), if

u\in C\big([0,T];L^{2}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k})\big)\cap L^{p}\big(0,T;W^{1,p}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k})\big)

and if the integral identity

\iint_{E_{T}}\Big[u\cdot\partial_{t}\zeta-a(x,t)\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}Du\cdot D\zeta\Big]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=0

is satisfied for any test function

\zeta\in W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k}))\cap L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}_{0}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k}))

such that
\zeta(\cdot,0)=\zeta(\cdot,T)=0
.

A notion of local solution is commonly used in the literature, cf.
[
17
, Chapter VIII]
. We stress that such notion makes no essential difference from Definition
2.6
modulo a
localization.

3.
Gradient bound for
p
-Laplace systems with differentiable coefficients

In this section, we provide a sup-bound for the spatial gradient of solutions to parabolic
p
-Laplace-type systems with differentiable coefficients. More precisely, let us consider weak solutions to

(3.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u-\Div\Big(b(x,t)\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}Du\Big)=0\quad\mbox{in $E_{T}$,}

where
p>1
and
\mu\in(0,1]
. For the coefficients
b
we assume that the map
E\ni x\mapsto b(x,t)
is differentiable for almost every
t\in(0,T)
, that
(0,T)\ni t\mapsto b(x,t)
is measurable for every
x\in E
, and finally that

(3.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}C_{o}\leq b(x,t)\leq C_{1},\\[5.0pt]
|\nabla b(x,t)|\leq C_{2},\end{array}\right.

for every
x\in E
and almost every
t\in(0,T)
,
with constants
0<C_{o}\leq C_{1}
and
C_{2}\geq 0
.

3.1.
Energy estimates for second order derivatives

The starting point for gradient boundedness is an energy inequality involving second order weak derivatives of solutions. The proof can be found in
[
11
, Proposition 3.2]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mu\in(0,1]
and
p>1
, and assume that assumptions (
3.2
) are in force.
Then there exists a constant
C=C(p,C_{o},C_{1},C_{2})
such that whenever
u
is a weak solution to the parabolic system (
3.1
) in the sense of Definition
2.6
, for any non-negative, non-decreasing, bounded, Lipschitz function
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and every parabolic cylinder
Q_{R,S}(z_{o})=B_{R}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-S,t_{o}]\subset E_{T}
with
R,S>0
and every non-negative cut-off function
\zeta\in C^{\infty}(Q_{R,S}(z_{o}),\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
vanishing on
\partial_{\mathrm{par}}Q_{R,S}(z_{o})
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in(t_{o}-S,t_{o}]}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}(x_{o})\times\{\tau\}}v\,\zeta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|D^{2}u|^{2}\Phi(|Du|^{2})\zeta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\cap\{\Phi(|Du|^{2})>0\}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\frac{\Phi^{2}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{2}}{\Phi(|Du|^{2})+\Phi^{\prime}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{2}}\big(\zeta^{2}+|\nabla\zeta|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\Phi^{\prime}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{4}\zeta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}v\big|\partial_{t}\zeta^{2}\big|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

where

(3.4)

v:=\int_{0}^{|Du|^{2}}\Phi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

The next lemma essentially follows from Proposition
3.1
by choosing
\Phi(\tau)=(\delta^{2}+\tau)^{\alpha}
. However, since
\Phi
is not a bounded Lipschitz function we have to choose a truncated version instead.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
p>1
and
\mu\in(0,1]
, and let
u

be a weak solution to the parabolic system (
3.1
) in the sense of Definition
2.6
. Moreover, assume that the hypotheses (
3.2
) are in force and that for some
\alpha\geq 0
the solution satisfies on some cylinder
Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\Subset E_{T}
with
R\in(0,1]
and
S>0
the integrability condition

|Du|\in L^{p+2\alpha}\big(Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\big)\cap L^{2+2\alpha}\big(Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\big).

Then, for every
0<r<R
,
0<s<S
, and every
\delta\in[0,1]
we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}\sup_{t\in(t_{o}-s,t_{o}]}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(x_{o})\times\{t\}}\big(\delta^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{1+\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\iint_{Q_{r,s}(z_{o})}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\big(\delta^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\alpha}|D^{2}u|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(1+\alpha)}{(R-r)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\big(\delta^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\alpha+1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\frac{C}{S-s}\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\big(\delta^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{1+\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

for a constant
C=C(p,C_{o},C_{1},C_{2})
.

Proof..

We first consider the case
\delta>0
. In Proposition
3.1
we choose

\Phi_{m}(\tau):=\big(\delta^{2}+\min\{\tau,m\}\big)^{\alpha}\quad\mbox{for $\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$.}

Then,
\Phi_{m}
is positive, non-decreasing, bounded and Lipschitz continuous for every
m\geq 0
.
Moreover,
\Phi_{m}(\tau)\uparrow\Phi(\tau):=(\delta^{2}+\tau)^{\alpha}
in the limit
m\to\infty
. Therefore, Proposition
3.1
is applicable with
\Phi_{m}
instead of
\Phi
, and with a proper choice of
\zeta
the energy estimate implies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(t_{o}-s,t_{o}]}\int_{B_{r}(x_{o})\times\{t\}}v_{m}\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint_{Q_{r,s}(z_{o})}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|D^{2}u|^{2}\Phi_{m}(|Du|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{C}{(R-r)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\frac{\Phi_{m}^{2}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{2}}{\Phi_{m}(|Du|^{2})+\Phi_{m}^{\prime}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\Phi_{m}^{\prime}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{4}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\phantom{\leq\,}+\frac{C}{S-s}\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}v_{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

where

\displaystyle v_{m}:=\int_{0}^{|Du|^{2}}\Phi_{m}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

We note that
v_{m}\uparrow v
a.e. in the limit
m\to\infty
, where
v
is defined analogously with
\Phi
instead of
\Phi_{m}
.
Using either Fatou’s lemma or monotone convergence theorem, it follows that the integrals on the left-hand side in the limit
m\to\infty
converge to the corresponding integrals with
\Phi
instead of
\Phi_{m}
. For the last integral on the right-hand side this holds true again by monotone convergence theorem. For the second integral on the right, we have

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}

\displaystyle\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\Phi_{m}^{\prime}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{4}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\alpha\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\cap\{|Du|^{2}\leq m\}}\underbrace{\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\big(\delta^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\alpha-1}|Du|^{4}}_{\leq(1+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}+\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Since
|Du|\in L^{p+2\alpha}\big(Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\big)
, we conclude with the dominated convergence theorem that also these integrals converge in the limit
m\to\infty
. Therefore, it remains to treat the first integral on the right-hand side. Here we have

\displaystyle\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi_{m}^{2}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{2}}{\Phi_{m}(|Du|^{2})+\Phi_{m}^{\prime}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\big(\delta^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\alpha}|Du|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\big(1+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}+\alpha}\in L^{1}\big(Q_{R,S}(z_{o})).

Therefore, we can pass to the limit
m\to\infty
in these integrals as well. Finally, the function
v
takes the form

v=\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}\big(\delta^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{1+\alpha}-\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}\delta^{2(1+\alpha)}.

We move the integral over
B_{R}(x_{o})
of the negative part to the right-hand side. Furthermore we estimate this integral simply by

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}\delta^{2(1+\alpha)}|B_{R}|

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{(1+\alpha)S}\delta^{2(1+\alpha)}|Q_{R,S}|\leq\frac{1}{S-s}\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\big(\delta^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{1+\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

This completes the proof in the case
\delta>0
. If
\delta=0
we proceed as before with some
\tilde{\delta}>0
instead of
\delta
and then let
\tilde{\delta}\downarrow 0
.
∎

3.2.
L^{m}
-gradient estimates in the sub-quadratic case

Our goal is to implement a Moser iteration procedure to prove the local boundedness of the gradient for any
p>1
. However, in the so-called sub-critical case
p\leq\frac{2N}{N+2}
, this is only possible for solutions that satisfy the additional regularity property
|Du|\in L^{m}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T})
for a sufficiently large power
m>p
. The latter can be shown for
bounded
solutions using ideas from
[
17
, Chapter VIII, Lemma 4.1]
, see also
[
20
,
15
]
. In what follows, we present the proof for all
1<p\leq 2
in a unified fashion.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\mu\in(0,1]
and
1<p\leq 2
. Then, for any locally bounded weak solution to the parabolic system (
3.1
) in the sense of Definition
2.6
, we have
|Du|\in L^{m}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T})
for every
m>1
.
Moreover, for every
m>3
,
we have for every pair of concentric cylinders
Q_{r,s}(z_{o})\subset Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\Subset E_{T}
with
0<r<R\leq 1
and
0<s<S

the quantitative estimate

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{r,s}(z_{o})}

\displaystyle|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(3.5)

\displaystyle\leq\max\bigg\{C\varepsilon^{\frac{(p-2)(m-p)}{p}}\bigg[\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\bm{\omega}}{R-r}+\Big(\frac{m\bm{\omega}}{\sqrt{S-s}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\bigg]^{m-p}\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}|Du|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\max\bigg\{\,\,}\varepsilon^{m}\mu^{m}|Q_{R,S}|\bigg\}

for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, with a constant
C=C(N,p,C_{o},C_{1},C_{2})
. In (
3.3
) we denoted

\bm{\omega}:=\sup_{t\in(t_{o}-S,t_{o}]}\sup_{x\in B_{R}(x_{o})}\big|u(x,t)-(u)_{x_{o},R}(t)\big|,

and

(u)_{x_{o},R}(t)=\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}\!\int_{B_{R}(x_{o})}u(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof..

We omit the base point
z_{o}
from the notation for simplicity.
To start with, we show

|Du|\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T}).

This can be achieved by adapting
the difference quotient technique as illustrated in the appendix of
[
15
]
. We briefly sketch the argument by giving the formal computations. We consider two concentric cylinders
Q_{r_{1},s_{1}}\subset Q_{r_{2},s_{2}}
with
r\leq r_{1}<r_{2}\leq R
and
s\leq s_{1}<s_{2}\leq S
, and define
\varrho:=\frac{1}{2}(r_{1}+r_{2})
. Moreover, we let
\zeta\in C^{1}_{0}\big(B_{\varrho},[0,1]\big)
be a cut-off function in space such that
\zeta=1
on
B_{r_{1}}
and
|\nabla\zeta|\leq\frac{2}{\varrho-r_{1}}=\frac{4}{r_{2}-r_{1}}
.
First, we note that integration by parts yields

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{r_{1},s_{1}}

\displaystyle:=\iint_{Q_{r_{1},s_{1}}}|Du|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\leq\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}|Du|^{2}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}D[u-(u)_{R}(t)]\cdot Du\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}[u-(u)_{R}(t)]\cdot\Delta u\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t-\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}[u-(u)_{R}(t)]\cdot Du\nabla\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(3.6)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{N}\bm{\omega}\,\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}|D^{2}u|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\frac{4\bm{\omega}}{r_{2}-r_{1}}\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}|Du|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

For the estimate of the first integral on the right-hand side, we apply Hölder’s inequality with exponents
2
,
\frac{2p}{2-p}
, and
\frac{p}{p-1}

to obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}|D^{2}u|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{4}}|D^{2}u|\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{2-p}{4}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{I}_{\varrho,s_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{2-p}{2p}}|Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}|^{\frac{p-1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{I}_{\varrho,s_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg[\underbrace{\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t}_{=:\mathsf{E}}\bigg]^{\frac{2-p}{2p}}|Q_{R,S}|^{\frac{p-1}{p}},

where

\mathbf{I}_{\varrho,s_{1}}:=\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|D^{2}u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

To estimate
\mathbf{I}_{\varrho,s_{1}}
we apply the energy estimate from Lemma
3.2
with
\alpha=0
,
\delta=0
and with
(r,s)
,
(R,S)
replaced by
(\varrho,s_{1})
,
(r_{2},s_{2})
. This yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{\varrho,s_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}\underbrace{\iint_{Q_{r_{2},s_{2}}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t}_{\leq\,\mathsf{E}}+\frac{C\,\bm{\phi}_{r_{2},s_{2}}}{s_{2}-s_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\,\mathsf{E}}{(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}+\frac{C\,\bm{\phi}_{r_{2},s_{2}}}{s_{2}-s_{1}}.

We use this inequality to bound the right-hand side in the estimate of
|D^{2}u|
and obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}|D^{2}u|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg[\frac{\mathsf{E}}{(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}+\frac{\bm{\phi}_{r_{2},s_{2}}}{s_{2}-s_{1}}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{E}^{\frac{2-p}{2p}}|Q_{R,S}|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg[\frac{\mathsf{E}^{\frac{1}{p}}}{r_{2}-r_{1}}+\frac{\bm{\phi}_{r_{2},s_{s}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{E}^{\frac{2-p}{2p}}}{(s_{2}-s_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\bigg]|Q_{R,S}|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}.

To estimate the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.2
), we multiply the above inequality by
C\bm{\omega}
and apply Young’s inequality to the individual terms. In this way we get

\displaystyle C\bm{\omega}\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}|D^{2}u|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\bm{\phi}_{r_{2},s_{2}}+C\bigg[\frac{\bm{\omega}\mathsf{E}^{\frac{1}{p}}}{r_{2}-r_{1}}|Q_{R,S}|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}+\frac{\bm{\omega}^{2}\mathsf{E}^{\frac{2-p}{p}}}{s_{2}-s_{1}}|Q_{R,S}|^{2(1-\frac{1}{p})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\bm{\phi}_{r_{2},s_{2}}+\frac{C\bm{\omega}|Q_{R,S}|}{r_{2}-r_{1}}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad\,\,\,\,+\frac{C\bm{\omega}^{2}|Q_{R,S}|}{s_{2}-s_{1}}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{2-p}{p}}.

Together with the estimate

\frac{4\bm{\omega}}{r_{2}-r_{1}}\iint_{Q_{\varrho,s_{1}}}|Du|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{4\bm{\omega}|Q_{R,S}|}{r_{2}-r_{1}}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}},

this allows us to bound the right-hand side of (
3.2
), so that

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{r_{1},s_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\bm{\phi}_{r_{2},s_{2}}+C\frac{\bm{\omega}^{2}|Q_{R,S}|}{s_{2}-s_{1}}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{2-p}{p}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\,\,\;+C\frac{\bm{\omega}|Q_{R,S}|}{r_{2}-r_{1}}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}.

In view of the iteration Lemma
2.2
, we can re-absorb the first term on the
right-hand side and obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{r,s}}|Du|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\bm{\omega}^{2}|Q_{R,S}|}{S-s}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{2-p}{p}}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\frac{C\bm{\omega}|Q_{R,S}|}{R-r}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}},

where the right-hand side is finite by assumption. Therefore, we have shown (modulo a suitable difference quotient argument) that
|Du|\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T})
.

Now, let
m\geq p+1>2
. We start by assuming that

|Du|\in L^{m-p+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T}),

which holds true for
m=p+1
since
|Du|\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T})
.

As in the first part of the proof, consider concentric cylinders
Q_{r,s}\subset Q_{R,S}
with
0<r<R
and
0<s<S
, and let
\varrho:=\frac{1}{2}(R+r)
and
\theta:=\frac{1}{2}(S+s)
. Moreover, let
\zeta\in C^{1}_{0}(B_{\varrho},[0,1])
be a cut-off function in space such that
\zeta=1
on
B_{r}
and
|\nabla\zeta|\leq\frac{2}{\varrho-r}=\frac{4}{R-r}
.
Similarly to the first part of the proof, we calculate by integrating by parts (modulo the difference quotient technique)

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}

\displaystyle|Du|^{m}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}D_{\gamma}\big[u-u_{R}(t)\big]\cdot D_{\gamma}u|Du|^{m-2}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}\underbrace{\big[u-u_{R}(t)\big]}_{|\cdot|\,\leq\,\bm{\omega}}\cdot D_{\gamma}\big[D_{\gamma}u|Du|^{m-2}\zeta\big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\bm{\omega}\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}\big|D_{\gamma}\big[D_{\gamma}u|Du|^{m-2}\big]\big|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\frac{4\bm{\omega}}{R-r}\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}|Du|^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

To estimate the first integral on the right-hand side, we observe that

\displaystyle\big|D_{\gamma}\big[D_{\gamma}u|Du|^{m-2}\big]\big|

\displaystyle=\Big|D_{\gamma}D_{\gamma}u|Du|^{m-2}+D_{\gamma}uD_{\gamma}|Du|^{m-2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq|Du|^{m-2}\bigg|D_{\gamma}D_{\gamma}u+(m-2)\frac{D_{\gamma}uD_{\gamma}D_{\beta}u\cdot D_{\beta}u}{|Du|^{2}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq|Du|^{m-2}\Big[\sqrt{N}|D^{2}u|+(m-2)|D^{2}u|\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{N}(m-1)|Du|^{m-2}|D^{2}u|.

Using Hölder’s inequality we have

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}

\displaystyle|Du|^{m-2}|D^{2}u|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{4}}|Du|^{\frac{m-p-1}{2}}|D^{2}u|\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{2-p}{4}}|Du|^{\frac{m+p-3}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(3.7)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{I}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathbf{I}_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}},

where we abbreviated

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|^{m-p-1}|D^{2}u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{2-p}{2}}|Du|^{m+p-3}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Note that,
m+p-3\geq 2(p-1)>0
. Therefore, we have
\mathbf{I}_{2}<\infty
, due to the assumption
|Du|\in L^{m-p+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T})
and since
m-1\leq m-p+1
. The first integral
\mathbf{I}_{1}
can be controlled with the help of the energy inequality from Lemma
3.2
. In fact, taking
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}(m-p-1)
we have
p+2\alpha=m-1
, and
2+2\alpha=m-p+1
, so that the required integrability is fulfilled. Applying the energy inequality on
Q_{\varrho,\theta}
,
Q_{R,S}
and with
\delta=0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Cm}{(R-r)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|^{m-p+1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\frac{C}{S-s}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-p+1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(3.8)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Cm}{(R-r)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\frac{C}{S-s}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-p+1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

For the second integral, we estimate

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{2-p}{2}}|Du|^{m+p-3}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\mu^{2-p}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m+p-3}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Inserting this above and noting that
\zeta=1
on
Q_{r,s}
we arrive at

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{r,s}}

\displaystyle|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq Cm\bm{\omega}\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}|Du|^{m-2}|D^{2}u|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\frac{4\bm{\omega}}{R-r}\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}|Du|^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq Cm\bm{\omega}\Bigg[\frac{m}{(R-r)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{S-s}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-p+1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}

(3.9)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\Bigg[\mu^{2-p}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m+p-3}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\frac{4\bm{\omega}}{R-r}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

To obtain the last line we used the estimates (
3.2
) and (
3.2
). Observe that the right-hand side is finite by the assumption
|Du|\in L^{m-p+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T})\cap L^{m-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T})
. By means of an induction argument it is now easy to show that
|Du|\in L^{m}_{\rm loc}(E_{T})
for every
m>1
. Indeed, starting with

m=p+1
, we have
m-1=p
and
m-p+1=2
. Therefore, the assumption
|Du|\in L^{p}(Q_{R,S})\cap L^{2}(Q_{R,S})
is fulfilled as already mentioned. This implies that
|Du|\in L^{p+1}(Q_{r,s})
, and by a covering argument we have
|Du|\in L^{p+1}_{\rm loc}(E_{T})
. We now define
m_{\ell}:=p+1+\ell(p-1)
. Then,
m_{\ell+1}-p+1=m_{\ell}
and
m_{\ell+1}-1=m_{\ell}+p-2\leq m_{\ell}
. Therefore, the requirement
|Du|\in L^{m_{\ell+1}-p+1}(Q_{R,S})\cap L^{m_{\ell+1}-1}(Q_{R,S})
is fulfilled. This allows us to conclude that
|Du|\in L^{m_{\ell+1}}_{\rm loc}(E_{T})
. Since
m_{\ell}\uparrow\infty
, the assertion, i.e. that
|Du|\in L^{m}_{\rm loc}(E_{T})
for any
m>1
, is proven, and it remains to establish the quantitative
L^{m}
-bound.

To obtain the quantitative
L^{m}
-estimate (
3.3
), we further estimate various integrals on the right-hand side of (
3.2
) by interpolating between
L^{m}
and
L^{p}
norms. Indeed, by splitting the exponent
m-1
in the form

m-1=\frac{m(m-p-1)}{m-p}+\frac{p}{m-p},

we apply Hölder’s inequality with exponents
\frac{m-p}{m-p-1}
and
m-p
to obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{\frac{m(m-p-1)}{m-p}}|Du|^{\frac{p}{m-p}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{m-p-1}{m-p}}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{m-p}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{m-p-1}{m-p}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{1}{m-p}},

with the obvious meaning of
\mathfrak{D}_{m}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{p}
.
Similarly, splitting

m-p+1=\frac{m(m-2p+1)}{m-p}+\frac{p(p-1)}{m-p},

we find using Hölder’s inequality with exponents
\frac{m-p}{m-2p+1}
and
\frac{m-p}{p-1}
that

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m-p+1}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{\frac{m(m-2p+1)}{m-p}}|Du|^{\frac{p(p-1)}{m-p}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{m-2p+1}{m-p}}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{p-1}{m-p}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{m-2p+1}{m-p}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{p-1}{m-p}}.

Moreover, since

\frac{(m+p-3)(m-p)-p}{m(m-p)}+\frac{1}{m-p}+\frac{2-p}{m}=1

we have

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{R,S}}

\displaystyle|Du|^{m+p-3}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{\frac{(m+p-3)(m-p)-p}{m-p}}|Du|^{\frac{p}{m-p}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{(m+p-3)(m-p)-p}{m(m-p)}}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{m-p}}|Q_{R,S}|^{\frac{2-p}{m}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{(m+p-3)(m-p)-p}{m(m-p)}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{1}{m-p}}|Q_{R,S}|^{\frac{2-p}{m}}.

This is the step in the proof where we need the assumption
m>3
.
Without any loss of generality, we can assume that

(3.10)

\varepsilon^{m}\mu^{m}<\frac{1}{|Q_{R,S}|}\iint_{Q_{r,s}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

holds true, because otherwise (
3.3
) trivially holds.
This allows us to estimate

\displaystyle\mu^{2-p}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m+p-3}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{p-2}\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{(m+p-3)(m-p)-p}{m(m-p)}+\frac{2-p}{m}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{1}{m-p}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{p-2}\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{m-p-1}{m-p}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{1}{m-p}}.

Hence, substituting these estimates in (
3.2
) and applying Young’s inequality, we get

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{r,s}}

\displaystyle|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq Cm\varepsilon^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\bm{\omega}\Bigg[\frac{m\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{m-p-1}{m-p}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{1}{m-p}}}{(R-r)^{2}}+\frac{\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{m-2p+1}{m-p}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{p-1}{m-p}}}{S-s}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Big[\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{m-p-1}{m-p}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{1}{m-p}}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\frac{4\bm{\omega}}{R-r}\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{m-p-1}{m-p}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{1}{m-p}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Cm^{\frac{3}{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\bm{\omega}}{R-r}\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{m-p-1}{m-p}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{1}{m-p}}+\frac{Cm\varepsilon^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\bm{\omega}}{\sqrt{S-s}}\mathfrak{D}_{m}^{\frac{2m-3p}{2(m-p)}}\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{\frac{p}{2(m-p)}}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathfrak{D}_{m}+C\bigg[\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\bm{\omega}}{R-r}+\Big(\frac{m\varepsilon^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\bm{\omega}}{\sqrt{S-s}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\bigg]^{m-p}\mathfrak{D}_{p}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\varepsilon^{\frac{(p-2)(m-p)}{p}}\bigg[\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\bm{\omega}}{R-r}+\Big(\frac{m\bm{\omega}}{\sqrt{S-s}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\bigg]^{m-p}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

We apply this inequality on the cylinders
Q_{\sigma,\tau}
,
Q_{\varrho,\theta}

with
r\leq\sigma<\varrho\leq R
and
s\leq\tau<\theta\leq S
. To this end, we must ensure that (
3.10
) is also fulfilled on these cylinders, i.e. that

\varepsilon^{m}\mu^{m}|Q_{\varrho,\theta}|<\iint_{Q_{\sigma,\tau}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

holds true. However, this is implied by
(
3.10
).
Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{m}\mu^{m}|Q_{\varrho,\theta}|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{m}\mu^{m}|Q_{R,S}|<\iint_{Q_{r,s}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\leq\iint_{Q_{\sigma,\tau}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{\sigma,\tau}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\varepsilon^{\frac{(p-2)(m-p)}{p}}\bigg[\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\bm{\omega}}{\varrho-\sigma}+\Big(\frac{m\bm{\omega}}{\sqrt{\theta-\tau}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\bigg]^{m-p}\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}|Du|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

The iteration Lemma
2.2
implies

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{r,s}}|Du|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{(p-2)(m-p)}{p}}\bigg[\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\bm{\omega}}{R-r}+\Big(\frac{m\bm{\omega}}{\sqrt{S-s}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\bigg]^{m-p}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Together with (
3.10
) this results in
(
3.3
) and concludes the proof.
∎

3.3.
Qualitative gradient bound in the sub-quadratic case

The next result upgrades the previous higher integrability of the gradient and shows that locally bounded weak solutions have bounded gradient. Moser’s iteration scheme is employed here.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\mu\in(0,1]
and
1<p\leq 2
. Then, for any locally bounded weak solution
u
to the parabolic system (
3.1
) in the sense of Definition
2.6
with assumption (
3.2
), we have

|Du|\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T}).

Proof..

The starting point is the energy estimate from Lemma
3.2
applied with
\delta=\mu
on standard cylinders
Q_{R}(z_{o}):=Q_{R,R^{2}}(z_{o})
, i.e. for
0<r<R
such that
Q_{R}(z_{o})\Subset E_{T}
. For simplicity we take
z_{o}=(0,0)
; we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}\sup_{t\in(-r^{2},0]}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}\times\{t\}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{1+\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint_{Q_{r}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}+\alpha}|D^{2}u|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(1+\alpha)}{(R-r)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{R}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}+\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(3.11)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\frac{C}{(R-r)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{R}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{1+\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Since
1<p\leq 2
, the dominant term on the right-hand side is the integral with exponent
1+\alpha
, whereas the other term is controlled via

\displaystyle\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}+\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{1+\alpha}+1.

Hence, by Kato’s inequality, for any
\alpha\geq 0
we
have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}\sup_{t\in(-r^{2},0]}\int_{B_{r}\times\{t\}}

\displaystyle\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{1+\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint_{Q_{r}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}+\alpha}\big|\nabla|Du|\big|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(1+\alpha)}{(R-r)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{R}}\Big[\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{1+\alpha}+1\Big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

where
C=C(p,C_{o},C_{1},C_{2})
. Introduce

w:=\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{4}}

and rewrite the preceding estimate as

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}\sup_{t\in(-r^{2},0]}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}\times\{t\}}w^{\frac{4+4\alpha}{p+2\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\tfrac{1}{(p+2\alpha)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{r}}|\nabla w|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(3.12)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(1+\alpha)}{(R-r)^{2}}\iint_{Q_{R}}\big[w^{\frac{4+4\alpha}{p+2\alpha}}+1\big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

The above inequality serves as the starting point of the Moser iteration scheme. For
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we introduce the following abbreviations

(3.13)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle\varrho_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}R+\frac{R}{2^{i+1}},\quad\tilde{\varrho}_{i}=\frac{\varrho_{i}+\varrho_{i+1}}{2},\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle B_{i}=B_{\varrho_{i}},\quad Q_{i}=Q_{\varrho_{i}}=B_{i}\times(-\varrho_{i}^{2},0],\\[6.0pt]
\widetilde{B}_{i}=B_{\tilde{\varrho}_{i}},\quad\widetilde{Q}_{i}=Q_{\tilde{\varrho}_{i}}=\widetilde{B}_{i}\times(-\tilde{\varrho}_{i}^{2},0],\\[6.0pt]
\kappa=1+\frac{2}{N},\quad\alpha_{o}=\tfrac{1}{2}p_{o}-1,\quad\alpha_{i+1}=\kappa\alpha_{i}+\frac{2}{N}-\frac{2-p}{2},\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle p_{i}=2+2\alpha_{i},\quad q_{i}=\frac{4+4\alpha_{i}}{p+2\alpha_{i}},\end{array}\right.

where the exponent
p_{o}
is chosen such that

p_{o}>\max\big\{\frac{N(2-p)}{2},2\big\}
.
For the sequences
\alpha_{i}
and
p_{i}
it can be directly deduced from the recursion formulas that

\alpha_{i}=\tfrac{\bm{\nu}}{4}\kappa^{i}-1+\tfrac{N(2-p)}{4}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\bm{\nu}:=N(p-2)+2p_{o}>0

and

(3.14)

p_{i+1}=(p+2\alpha_{i})\tfrac{N+q_{i}}{N}

hold true for any
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Note also that

p_{i}=\tfrac{\bm{\nu}}{2}\kappa^{i}+\tfrac{N(2-p)}{2}.

To proceed further
let
\zeta_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\widetilde{Q}_{i},[0,1])
be a standard cut-off function that
vanishes on
\partial_{\mathrm{par}}\widetilde{Q}_{i}
and equals one in
Q_{i+1}
,
such that
|\nabla\zeta_{i}|\leq 2^{i+4}/R
.
We apply the Sobolev imbedding
[
17
, Chapter I, Proposition 3.1]
with
p=2
and
m=q_{i}
,
to obtain that

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{i+1}}

\displaystyle\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p_{i+1}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\leq\iint_{\widetilde{Q}_{i}}(w\zeta_{i})^{2\frac{N+q_{i}}{N}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{\widetilde{Q}_{i}}|\nabla(w\zeta_{i})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg[\sup_{\tau\in(-\tilde{\varrho}_{i}^{2},0]}\int_{\widetilde{B}_{i}\times\{\tau\}}(w\zeta_{i})^{q_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg]^{\frac{2}{N}}

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{\widetilde{Q}_{i}}\big[\zeta_{i}^{2}|\nabla w|^{2}+w^{2}|\nabla\zeta_{i}|^{2}\big]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg[\sup_{\tau\in(-\tilde{\varrho}_{i}^{2},0]}\int_{\widetilde{B}_{i}\times\{\tau\}}w^{q_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg]^{\frac{2}{N}}

(3.15)

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{\widetilde{Q}_{i}}\bigg[|\nabla w|^{2}+\frac{2^{2i+8}w^{2}}{R^{2}}\bigg]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg[\sup_{\tau\in(-\tilde{\varrho}_{i}^{2},0]}\int_{\widetilde{B}_{i}\times\{\tau\}}w^{q_{i}}\mathrm{d}x\bigg]^{\frac{2}{N}},

for a constant
C=C(N)
. In turn we used the properties of the cut-off function
\zeta_{i}
. The fact that the constant actually depends only on
N
and
p
can be seen as follows. Applying the Sobolev embedding from the first to second line yields a constant depending on
N
and
q_{i}
.
Since
q_{i}=2+\frac{2(2-p)}{p+2\alpha_{i}}\in[2,4]
and because the constant
C_{\rm Sob}(N,q_{i})
from the Sobolev embedding depends continuously on
q_{i}
, the dependence on
q_{i}
can be eliminated.
To the right-hand side integrals of (
3.3
) we apply the energy estimate (
3.3
) on
\widetilde{Q}_{i}\subset Q_{i}
instead of
Q_{r}\subset Q_{R}
, and with
\alpha_{i}
in place of
\alpha
. This yields

\displaystyle\iint_{\widetilde{Q}_{i}}|\nabla w|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(p+2\alpha_{i})^{2}(1+\alpha_{i})\frac{2^{2i+6}}{R^{2}}\iint_{Q_{i}}\big[w^{q_{i}}+1\big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(1+\alpha_{i})^{3}2^{2i}}{R^{2}}\iint_{Q_{i}}\big[w^{q_{i}}+1\big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

and

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in(-\tilde{\varrho}_{i}^{2},0]}\int_{\widetilde{B}_{i}\times\{\tau\}}w^{q_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(1+\alpha_{i})^{2}2^{2i}}{R^{2}}\iint_{Q_{i}}\big[w^{q_{i}}+1\big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

The term
w^{2}
in (
3.3
) is estimated by means of

w^{2}\leq w^{q_{i}}+1
.
Using also
1+\alpha_{i}\leq C(N,p_{o})\kappa^{i}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{i+1}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p_{i+1}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\alpha_{i})^{3+\frac{4}{N}}\bigg[\frac{2^{2i}}{R^{2}}\iint_{Q_{i}}\big[w^{q_{i}}+1\big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\leq C(4\kappa^{3})^{i\kappa}\bigg[\frac{1}{R^{2}}\iint_{Q_{i}}\big[w^{q_{i}}+1\big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=C\bm{b}^{i\kappa}\bigg[\frac{1}{R^{2}}\iint_{Q_{i}}\Big[\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p_{i}}{2}}+1\Big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\kappa}

with
\bm{b}=\bm{b}(N)=4\kappa^{3}
and
C=C(N,p,p_{o},C_{o},C_{1},C_{2})
. Taking into account
|Q_{i}|\leq 2^{N+2}|Q_{i+1}|
and
|Q_{i}|^{\frac{2}{N}}\leq\big(2^{N}R^{N+2}\big)^{\frac{2}{N}}=4R^{2\kappa}
we can pass to mean values on both sides to deduce that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{i+1}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p_{i+1}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\bm{b}^{i\kappa}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{i}}\Big[\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p_{i}}{2}}+1\Big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\kappa}

for a constant
C=C(N,p,p_{o},C_{o},C_{1},C_{2})
. Adding
1
to the left-hand side we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{i+1}}\Big[\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p_{i+1}}{2}}+1\Big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\bm{b}^{i\kappa}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{i}}\Big[\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p_{i}}{2}}+1\Big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\kappa}.

Using the abbreviation

\bm{Y}_{i}:=\left[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{i}}\Big[\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p_{i}}{2}}+1\Big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\right]^{\frac{1}{p_{i}}},

the last inequality can be put into the recursive inequality

(3.16)

\bm{Y}_{i+1}^{p_{i+1}}\leq\big(C\bm{b}^{i}\bm{Y}_{i}^{p_{i}}\big)^{\kappa}\qquad\forall\,i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Iterating (
3.16
), we get

(3.17)

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{i}^{p_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{i}C^{\kappa^{i-j+1}}\prod_{j=1}^{i}\bm{b}^{j\kappa^{i-j+1}}\bm{Y}_{o}^{p_{o}\kappa^{i}}

for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
. The right-hand side of (
3.17
) can be controlled by means of Lemma
2.1
. In fact, we first take both sides to the power
\frac{1}{p_{i}}
. Then, we re-write the products in the form

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{i}C^{\frac{\kappa^{i-j+1}}{p_{i}}}

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{i}\bm{b}^{\frac{j\kappa^{i-j+1}}{p_{i}}}\bm{Y}_{o}^{\frac{p_{o}\kappa^{i}}{p_{i}}}

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{i}C^{\frac{\kappa^{i-j+1}}{\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nu}(\kappa^{i}-1)}}\Bigg]^{\frac{\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nu}(\kappa^{i}-1)}{p_{i}}}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{i}\bm{b}^{\frac{j\kappa^{i-j+1}}{\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nu}(\kappa^{i}-1)}}\Bigg]^{\frac{\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nu}(\kappa^{i}-1)}{p_{i}}}\big[\bm{Y}_{o}\big]^{p_{o}\frac{\kappa^{i}}{p_{i}}}.

The exponents outside the brackets converge as
i\to\infty
, i.e. we
have

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nu}(\kappa^{i}-1)}{p_{i}}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nu}(\kappa^{i}-1)}{\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nu}\kappa^{i}+\tfrac{N(2-p)}{2}}=1

as well as

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{\kappa^{i}}{p_{i}}=\frac{2}{\bm{\nu}}.

Consequently, by virtue of Lemma
2.1
we get

\displaystyle\limsup_{i\to\infty}\bm{Y}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{i\to\infty}\prod_{j=1}^{i}C^{\frac{\kappa^{i-j+1}}{\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nu}(\kappa^{i}-1)}}\prod_{j=1}^{i}\bm{b}^{\frac{j\kappa^{i-j+1}}{\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nu}(\kappa^{i}-1)}}\bm{Y}_{o}^{\frac{2p_{o}}{\bm{\nu}}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\frac{2\kappa}{\bm{\nu}(\kappa-1)}}\bm{b}^{\frac{2\kappa^{2}}{\bm{\nu}(\kappa-1)^{2}}}\bm{Y}_{o}^{\frac{2p_{o}}{\bm{\nu}}}

\displaystyle=C^{\frac{N+2}{\bm{\nu}}}\bm{b}^{\frac{(N+2)^{2}}{2\bm{\nu}}}\bm{Y}_{o}^{\frac{2p_{o}}{\bm{\nu}}},

whence we deduce

\sup_{Q_{\frac{1}{2}R}}|Du|\leq C\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R}}\big[|Du|^{p_{o}}+1\big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\Bigg]^{\frac{2}{\bm{\nu}}}

with a constant
C=C(N,p,p_{o},C_{o},C_{1},C_{2})
. By Lemma
3.3
we have
|Du|\in L^{p_{o}}_{\rm loc}(E_{T})
. With a standard covering argument we conclude the proof.
∎

3.4.
Quantitative gradient bound
in the sub-quadratic case

Now we are in a position to refine the previous result and derive a quantitative gradient bound. The argument exploits a De Giorgi-type iteration.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\mu\in(0,1]
,
1<p<2
, and
r>1
that satisfies

\bm{\nu}_{r}:=N(p-2)+2r>0.

Then there exists a constant
C=C(N,p,r,C_{o},C_{1},C_{2})
such that for any locally bounded weak solution
u
to the parabolic system (
3.1
) in the sense of Definition
2.6
with assumption (
3.2
) we have

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}(z_{o})}|Du|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\max\Bigg\{\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{\frac{2(2-p)(N+2)}{\bm{\nu}_{r}}}}\frac{\big(R^{2}/S\big)^{\frac{N}{\bm{\nu}_{r}}}}{(1-\sigma)^{\frac{2(N+2)}{\bm{\nu}_{r}}}}\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}|Du|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{2}{\bm{\nu}_{r}}},\varepsilon\Big(\frac{S}{R^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2-p}},\varepsilon\mu\Bigg\}

for every
Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\Subset E_{T}
with
R\in(0,1]
,
S>0
, every
\sigma\in(0,1)
, and every
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.

Proof..

The starting point of the proof of the quantitative gradient bound is the energy estimate (
3.1
) from Proposition
3.1
in which we discard the non-negative term
\Phi^{\prime}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{2}
in the denominator of the first integral on the right-hand side. Let us apply this inequality with

\Phi(\tau):=\tau^{\frac{2-p}{2}}\big(\sqrt{\tau}-k\big)_{+}^{r},\quad\mbox{and $r>1$, $k>0$.}

The fact that
\Phi
is neither bounded nor Lipschitz is immaterial, since by Proposition
3.4
we have
|Du|\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(E_{T})
and we can replace
\Phi
by
\Phi_{m}(\tau):=\Phi(\min\{\tau,m\})
with

m>\|Du\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{R,S})}^{2}
.

Next, we aim to estimate
v
defined in (
3.4
) with this choice of
\Phi
. Notice that we only need to consider the case
|Du|>k
as
v=0
in the opposite case. Substituting
\sqrt{\tau}=s
and noting that
p<2
we estimate from below by

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{|Du|^{2}}\Phi(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau=\int_{k^{2}}^{|Du|^{2}}\tau^{\frac{2-p}{2}}\big(\sqrt{\tau}-k\big)_{+}^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=2\int_{k}^{|Du|}s^{2-p}(s-k)^{r}s\,\mathrm{d}s\geq 2k^{2-p}\int_{k}^{|Du|}(s-k)^{r+1}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{2k^{2-p}}{r+2}\big(|Du|-k\big)^{r+2}.

Similarly, we estimate from above by

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=2\int_{k}^{|Du|}s^{2-p}(s-k)^{r}s\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 2\int_{k}^{|Du|}s^{r+3-p}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{r+4-p}|Du|^{r+4-p}\leq\frac{2}{r+2}|Du|^{r+4-p}.

Altogether, this results in

\frac{2k^{2-p}}{r+2}\big(|Du|-k\big)_{+}^{r+2}\leq v\leq\frac{2}{r+2}|Du|^{r+4-p}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k\}}.

The second integral on the left-hand
side of (
3.1
), i.e. the one containing
|D^{2}u|^{2}
, can be estimated from below, if we require

k\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\mu
with
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.
In this case we have
|Du|>k\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\mu
on the set
\{|Du|>k\}
, which implies

\displaystyle\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|D^{2}u|^{2}\Phi\big(|Du|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k\}}\big(\underbrace{\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}}_{\leq 5\varepsilon^{-2}|Du|^{2}}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|^{2-p}\big(|Du|-k\big)^{r}|D^{2}u|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq 5^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\varepsilon^{2-p}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k\}}\big(|Du|-k\big)^{r}\big|\nabla|Du|\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\frac{4\,\varepsilon^{2-p}}{C(p)(r+2)^{2}}\Big|\nabla\big(|Du|-k\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\Big|^{2}.

In turn we used Kato’s inequality
|\nabla|Du||\leq|D^{2}u|
.

Finally, we consider the integrand of the first and second integrals appearing on the right-hand side of (
3.1
). Again we only have to focus on the set
\{|Du|>k\}
. Taking again into account that
p<2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\mu^{2}

\displaystyle+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|^{2}\Phi\big(|Du|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k\}}\underbrace{\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}_{\leq|Du|^{p-2}}|Du|^{2}|Du|^{2-p}\big(|Du|-k\big)^{r}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k\}}|Du|^{r+2}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\big(\mu^{2}

\displaystyle+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\Phi^{\prime}(|Du|^{2})|Du|^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k\}}|Du|^{2}\big(|Du|-k\big)^{r-1}\Big[\big(1-\tfrac{p}{2}+\tfrac{r}{2}\big)|Du|-\big(1-\tfrac{p}{2}\big)k\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(r+1)\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k\}}|Du|^{r+2}.

Inserting these inequalities into the energy inequality (
3.1
) (taking
z_{o}=(0,0)
for simplicity) results in

\displaystyle k^{2-p}\sup_{\tau\in(-S,0]}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}\times\{\tau\}}\big[\big(|Du|-k\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta\big]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\varepsilon^{2-p}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big|\nabla\big[\big(|Du|-k\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k\}}|Du|^{r+2}\big(|\nabla\zeta|^{2}+\zeta^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(3.18)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{r+4-p}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k\}}\big|\partial_{t}\zeta^{2}\big|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

with a constant
C=C(p,r,C_{o},C_{1},C_{2})
.
Note that the preceding energy inequality holds true for any general cylinder
Q_{R,S}
, for any
k\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\mu
, and any cut-off function
\zeta
vanishing on the parabolic boundary
\partial_{\mathrm{par}}Q_{R,S}
.

Now, for a fixed cylinder
Q_{o}:=Q_{R,S}
we define shrinking families
Q_{n}:=Q_{\varrho_{n},\theta_{n}}
and
\widetilde{Q}_{n}:=Q_{\tilde{\varrho}_{n},\tilde{\theta}_{n}}
of cylinders with the same base point, where

\varrho_{n}
,
\tilde{\varrho}_{n}
,
\theta_{n}
,
\tilde{\theta}_{n}
are defined according to

\varrho_{n}:=\sigma R+\frac{1-\sigma}{2^{n}}R,\quad\ \theta_{n}:=\sigma S+\frac{1-\sigma}{2^{n}}S

and

\tilde{\varrho}_{n}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\varrho_{n}+\varrho_{n+1}\big)=\sigma R+\frac{3(1-\sigma)}{2^{n+2}}R,\quad\tilde{\theta}_{n}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\theta_{n}+\theta_{n+1}\big)=\sigma S+\frac{3(1-\sigma)}{2^{n+2}}S.

These cylinders are arranged in such a way that
Q_{n+1}\subset\widetilde{Q}_{n}\subset Q_{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The level
k
in the energy estimate is fixed by an increasing sequence of levels

k_{n}:=k-\frac{k}{2^{n}},

where
k\geq\varepsilon\mu
is a quantity to be determined later in the course of the proof. Note that
k_{n+1}>k_{o}=\frac{1}{2}k\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon\mu
.
Since the above energy inequality shall be applied on the cylinders
Q_{n}
, we need to specify the cut-off function
\zeta
in such a way that it vanishes on the parabolic boundary of
Q_{n}
on the one hand, and is identical to 1 on the intermediate cylinder
\widetilde{Q}_{n}
on the other hand. In addition, we require that

|\nabla\zeta_{n}|\leq\frac{2^{n+3}}{(1-\sigma)R}\quad\mbox{and}\quad|\partial_{t}\zeta_{n}|\leq\frac{2^{n+3}}{(1-\sigma)S}.

With respect to these choices, the energy estimate (
3.4
) for
Q_{n}
with
k_{n+1}
reads as follows

\displaystyle k^{2-p}\sup_{\tau\in(-\theta_{n},0]}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{n}\times\{\tau\}}\big(|Du|-k_{n+1}\big)_{+}^{r+2}\zeta_{n}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\varepsilon^{2-p}\iint_{Q_{n}}\big|\nabla\big[\big(|Du|-k_{n+1}\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta_{n}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C2^{2n}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}R^{2}}\iint_{Q_{n}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}|Du|^{r+2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(3.19)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\frac{C2^{n}}{(1-\sigma)S}\iint_{Q_{n}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}|Du|^{r+4-p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

To shorten the notation introduce

\bm{Y}_{n}:=\iint_{Q_{n}}\big(|Du|-k_{n}\big)_{+}^{r+2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

The integral
\bm{Y}_{n}
can be easily estimated from below by reducing the domain of integration. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{n}}\big(|Du|-k_{n}\big)_{+}^{r+2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\iint_{\widetilde{Q}_{n}\cap\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}\big(|Du|-k_{n}\big)_{+}^{r+2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\big(k_{n+1}-k_{n}\big)^{r+2}\Big|\widetilde{Q}_{n}\cap\big\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\big\}\Big|

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{r+2}}{2^{(n+1)(r+2)}}\Big|\widetilde{Q}_{n}\cap\big\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\big\}\Big|.

This measure estimate can be used to estimate
\bm{Y}_{n+1}
from above. For this purpose we first increase in
\bm{Y}_{n+1}
the domain of integration from
Q_{n+1}
to
\widetilde{Q}_{n}
and then apply Hölder’s inequality with exponents
\frac{N+2}{N}
and
\frac{N+2}{2}
. Subsequently, we use the fact that
\zeta_{n}=1
on
\widetilde{Q}_{n}
and the measure estimate. In this way we get

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{\widetilde{Q}_{n}}\Big[\big(|Du|-k_{n+1}\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta_{n}\Big]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\iint_{\widetilde{Q}_{n}}\Big[\big(|Du|-k_{n+1}\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta_{n}\Big]^{2\frac{N+2}{N}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{N}{N+2}}\Big|\widetilde{Q}_{n}\cap\big\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\big\}\Big|^{\frac{2}{N+2}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\iint_{Q_{n}}\Big[\big(|Du|-k_{n+1}\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta_{n}\Big]^{2\frac{N+2}{N}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{N}{N+2}}\bigg[\frac{2^{(r+2)(n+1)}}{k^{r+2}}\bm{Y}_{n}\bigg]^{\frac{2}{N+2}}.

The first integral on the right-hand side can be controlled using Sobolev’s inequality
[
17
, Chapter I, Proposition 3.1]
with
m=p=2
and
q=2\frac{N+2}{N}
. The application yields

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{n}}

\displaystyle\Big[\big(|Du|-k_{n+1}\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta_{n}\Big]^{2\frac{N+2}{N}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\leq C\mathbf{I}\cdot\mathbf{II}^{\frac{2}{N}},

where we abbreviated

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}

\displaystyle:=\iint_{Q_{n}}\Big|\nabla\big[\big(|Du|-k_{n+1}\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta_{n}\big]\Big|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\leq\varepsilon^{p-2}\big[\mbox{right-hand side of \eqref{est:en-k_(n+1)}}\big]

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{II}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\tau\in(-\theta_{n},0]}\int_{B_{n}\times\{\tau\}}\big[\big(|Du|-k_{n+1}\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta_{n}\big]^{2}\mathrm{d}x\leq k^{p-2}\big[\mbox{right-hand side of \eqref{est:en-k_(n+1)}}\big].

Inserting this above gives

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{n}}\!\Big[\big(|Du|-k_{n+1}\big)_{+}^{\frac{r+2}{2}}\zeta_{n}\Big]^{2\frac{N+2}{N}}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{p-2}k^{(p-2)\frac{2}{N}}\big[\mbox{right-hand side of \eqref{est:en-k_(n+1)}}\big]^{\frac{N+2}{N}}.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\varepsilon^{p-2}k^{(p-2)\frac{2}{N}}\big[\mbox{right-hand side of \eqref{est:en-k_(n+1)}}\big]^{\frac{N+2}{N}}\Big]^{\frac{N}{N+2}}\bigg[\frac{2^{(r+2)(n+1)}}{k^{r+2}}\bm{Y}_{n}\bigg]^{\frac{2}{N+2}}

\displaystyle=C\varepsilon^{\frac{N(p-2)}{N+2}}k^{-\frac{2(r+4-p)}{N+2}}2^{\frac{2(r+2)(n+1)}{N+2}}\bm{Y}_{n}^{\frac{2}{N+2}}\big[\mbox{right-hand side of \eqref{est:en-k_(n+1)}}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C2^{n(r+4)}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{N(p-2)}{N+2}}k^{-\frac{2(r+4-p)}{N+2}}\bm{Y}_{n}^{\frac{2}{N+2}}\bm{E}_{n},

where we abbreviated

\displaystyle\bm{E}_{n}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{R^{2}}\iint_{Q_{n}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}|Du|^{r+2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{S}\iint_{Q_{n}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}|Du|^{r+4-p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Since
|Du|
is locally bounded in
E_{T}
, we can further estimate

\displaystyle\bm{E}_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\frac{1}{R^{2}}+\frac{1}{S}\Big(\sup_{Q_{n}}|Du|\Big)^{2-p}\bigg]\iint_{Q_{n}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}|Du|^{r+2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

so that only an integral with
|Du|^{r+2}
as integrand remains on the right-hand side. This integral can be controlled by
\bm{Y}_{n}
. In fact,
we shrink the domain of integration in
\bm{Y}_{n}
from
\{|Du|>k_{n}\}
to

\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}
and use

k_{n}=k_{n+1}\frac{2^{n+1}-2}{2^{n+1}-1},

to obtain

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{n}

\displaystyle\geq\iint_{Q_{n}}\big(|Du|-k_{n}\big)_{+}^{r+2}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{n}}\bigg[|Du|-k_{n+1}\frac{2^{n+1}-2}{2^{n+1}-1}\bigg]^{r+2}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\iint_{Q_{n}}|Du|^{r+2}\bigg[1-\frac{2^{n+1}-2}{2^{n+1}-1}\bigg]^{r+2}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2^{(n+1)(r+2)}}\iint_{Q_{n}}|Du|^{r+2}\mathbf{1}_{\{|Du|>k_{n+1}\}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Inserting this above we get

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C2^{2n(r+4)}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{N(p-2)}{N+2}}k^{-\frac{2(r+4-p)}{N+2}}\bigg[\frac{1}{R^{2}}+\frac{1}{S}\Big(\sup_{Q_{n}}|Du|\Big)^{2-p}\bigg]\bm{Y}_{n}^{1+\frac{2}{N+2}}.

Now, if for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have

\Big(\sup_{Q_{n}}|Du|\Big)^{2-p}\leq\varepsilon^{2-p}\frac{S}{R^{2}}

there is nothing more to prove, since

(3.20)

\sup_{Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}}|Du|=\sup_{Q_{\infty}}|Du|\leq\sup_{Q_{n}}|Du|\leq\varepsilon\Big(\frac{S}{R^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2-p}}.

Otherwise, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have

\Big(\sup_{Q_{n}}|Du|\Big)^{2-p}\geq\varepsilon^{2-p}\frac{S}{R^{2}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{R^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{S}\Big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\sup_{Q_{n}}|Du|\Big)^{2-p},

and from the above iterative inequality we obtain

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\bm{b}^{n}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}S}\varepsilon^{\frac{N(p-2)}{N+2}}k^{-\frac{2(r+4-p)}{N+2}}\Big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\sup_{Q_{o}}|Du|\Big)^{2-p}\bm{Y}_{n}^{1+\frac{2}{N+2}},

where we abbreviated
\bm{b}=4^{2(r+4)}
. At this point we apply the lemma on geometric convergence
[
17
, Chapter I, Lemma 4.1]
with
C
and
\alpha
replaced by

\frac{C\bm{}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}S}\varepsilon^{\frac{N(p-2)}{N+2}}k^{-\frac{2(r+4-p)}{N+2}}\Big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\sup_{Q_{o}}|Du|\Big)^{2-p}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tfrac{2}{N+2}.

Then, if

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{o}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{o}}|Du|^{r+2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg[\frac{C}{(1-\sigma)^{2}S}\varepsilon^{\frac{N(p-2)}{N+2}}k^{-\frac{2(r+4-p)}{N+2}}\Big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\sup_{Q_{o}}|Du|\Big)^{2-p}\Bigg]^{-\frac{N+2}{2}}\bm{b}^{-\frac{(N+2)^{2}}{4}}

\displaystyle=C^{-1}\big((1-\sigma)^{2}S\big)^{\frac{N+2}{2}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{N(p-2)}{2}}\Big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\sup_{Q_{o}}|Du|\Big)^{-\frac{(2-p)(N+2)}{2}}k^{r+4-p},

we have
\bm{Y}_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, which yields

\sup_{Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}}|Du|\leq k.

The condition for
k
encoded in this geometric convergence is

k\geq\frac{C\varepsilon^{\frac{N(p-2)}{2(r+4-p)}}}{[(1-\sigma)^{2}S]^{\frac{N+2}{2(r+4-p)}}}\Big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\sup_{Q_{o}}|Du|\Big)^{\frac{(2-p)(N+2)}{2(r+4-p)}}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{o}}|Du|^{r+2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{r+4-p}}.

Let us label the quantity on the right-hand side as
\bm{K}
.
Taking also into account the condition
k\geq\varepsilon\mu
that we set up earlier,
we end up with

\sup_{Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}}|Du|\leq\max\{\bm{K},\varepsilon\mu\}.

At this point,
we simplify the exponent of
\varepsilon
in the definition of
\bm{K}
by estimating

\displaystyle\frac{N(2-p)}{2(r+4-p)}+\frac{(2-p)(N+2)}{2(r+4-p)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2-p)(2N+2)}{2(r+4-p)}\leq\frac{2(2-p)(N+2)}{2(r+4-p)}

and obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}}|Du|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\bigg[\frac{C\varepsilon^{2(p-2)}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}S}\bigg]^{\frac{N+2}{2(r+4-p)}}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{r+2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{r+4-p}}\Big(\sup_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|\Big)^{\frac{(2-p)(N+2)}{2(r+4-p)}}\vee\varepsilon\mu.

Let us reduce the integral exponent from
r+2
to
r
while increasing the exponent of the sup-norm. Consequently, joining this with the alternative estimate (
3.20
) we get

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}}|Du|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\max\Bigg\{\bigg[\frac{C\varepsilon^{2(p-2)}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}S}\bigg]^{\frac{N+2}{2(r+4-p)}}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{r}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{r+4-p}}\Big(\sup_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|\Big)^{\frac{(2-p)(N+2)+4}{2(r+4-p)}},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,\,\varepsilon\Big(\frac{S}{R^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2-p}},\varepsilon\mu\Bigg\}.

To derive the desired
L^{\infty}
-gradient bound we rest upon the above estimate and set up an interpolation argument.
To this end, define a sequence of nested cylinders
\widehat{Q}_{n}=\widehat{Q}_{\varrho_{n},\theta_{n}}
with
\varrho_{o}=\sigma R
,
\theta_{o}=\sigma S
, and
with

\varrho_{n}:=\sigma R+(1-\sigma)R\sum_{j=1}^{n}2^{-j}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\theta_{n}:=\sigma S+(1-\sigma)S\sum_{j=1}^{n}2^{-j}

for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Observe that
\widehat{Q}_{o}=Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}
and
\widehat{Q}_{\infty}=Q_{R,S}
. Moreover, we define

\bm{M}_{n}:=\sup_{\widehat{Q}_{n}}|Du|.

Now, we apply the above gradient bound on two consecutive cylinders
\widehat{Q}_{n+1}\supset\widehat{Q}_{n}
instead of

Q_{R,S}\supset Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}
. This means that
\tau\in(0,1)
must be determined in such a way that
\tau\widehat{Q}_{n+1}=\widehat{Q}_{n}
,
i.e.
\tau\theta_{n+1}=\theta_{n}
. This is the same as

(1-\tau)^{2}\theta_{n+1}=\frac{(\theta_{n+1}-\theta_{n})^{2}}{\theta_{n+1}}=\frac{(1-\sigma)^{2}S2^{-2(n+1)}}{1-(1-\sigma)2^{-(n+1)}},

which implies

\frac{1}{(1-\tau)^{2}\theta_{n+1}}=2^{2(n+1)}\frac{1-(1-\sigma)2^{-(n+1)}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}S}\leq\frac{2^{2(n+1)}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}S}.

Moreover, we have

\frac{\theta_{n+1}}{\varrho_{n+1}^{2}}=\frac{S}{R^{2}}\Big[\sigma+(1-\sigma)\sum_{j=1}^{n}2^{-j}\Big]^{-1}\leq\frac{2S}{R^{2}}.

This yields recursive inequalities of the type

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\max\Bigg\{\bigg[\frac{C2^{2(n+1)}\varepsilon^{2(p-2)}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}S}\bigg]^{\frac{N+2}{2(r+4-p)}}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{r}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{r+4-p}}\bm{M}_{n+1}^{\frac{(2-p)(N+2)+4}{2(r+4-p)}},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\,2^{\frac{1}{2-p}}\varepsilon\Big(\frac{S}{R^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2-p}},\varepsilon\mu\Bigg\}.

Note that the factor
2^{\frac{1}{2-p}}
in the second term can be avoided if we replace
\varepsilon
by
2^{-\frac{1}{2-p}}\varepsilon
.

If for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
either the second or the third term in the maximum dominates the first one, there is nothing to prove, because we trivially have

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}}|Du|=\bm{M}_{o}\leq\bm{M}_{n}\leq\max\bigg\{\varepsilon\Big(\frac{S}{R^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2-p}},\varepsilon\mu\bigg\}.

Otherwise, we have

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\Bigg[\frac{C\varepsilon^{2(p-2)}}{(1-\sigma)^{2}S}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{r}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{2}{N+2}}\Bigg]^{\frac{N+2}{2(r+4-p)}}}_{=:\kappa}\big(\underbrace{2^{\frac{N+2}{r+4-p}}}_{=:\bm{b}}\big)^{n}\bm{M}_{n+1}^{\frac{(2-p)(N+2)+4}{2(r+4-p)}}

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let

\displaystyle\bm{\nu}_{r}:=2r+N(p-2)>0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\alpha:=\frac{\bm{\nu}_{r}}{2(r+4-p)}\in(0,1).

Note that the exponent of
\bm{M}_{n+1}
is

\displaystyle\frac{(2-p)(N+2)+4}{2(r+4-p)}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\bm{\nu}_{r}}{2(r+4-p)}=1-\alpha.

With these abbreviations, the iterative inequalities have the form

\bm{M}_{n}\leq\kappa\bm{b}^{n}\bm{M}_{n+1}^{1-\alpha}.

The Interpolation Lemma
[
17
, Chapter I, Lemma 4.3]
yields

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{o}

\displaystyle=\sup_{Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}}|Du|
