, where the white regions give a graphical representation of the well-studied ranges.

Figure 2.

The theory becomes even more fragmented when it comes to gradient regularity.
The well-studied case is the parabolic
p
-Laplace equation starting from DiBenedetto & Friedman
[
18
,
20
]
. Indeed, the gradient of weak solutions is Hölder continuous.

On the other hand, gradient regularity for solutions to the porous medium equation is more involved and much less understood.
In the slow diffusion regime (
p=2
&
q<1
), only the one-dimensional case is reasonably understood. Aronson studied the Lipschitz continuity of
x\mapsto u^{1-q}(x,t)

in
[
3
]
. For
0<q\leq\frac{1}{2}
DiBenedetto
[
16
]
investigated the Lipschitz continuity of
t\mapsto u^{1-q}(x,t)
. The full range
0<q<1
has later been established by Bénilan
[
5
]
and Aronson & Caffarelli
[
4
]
.
In the same regime, the optimal regularity in the multi-dimensional case is still a major open problem.
Caffarelli & Vázquez & Wolanski
[
12
]
proved for a solution
u
to the Cauchy problem that
\nabla u^{1-q}
becomes bounded after some waiting time. This result was localized and improved for
0<q\leq\frac{1}{2}
by Gianazza & Siljander
[
22
]
who were, roughly speaking, able to quantify the waiting time. More precisely, if at some point
(x_{o},t_{o})
the average
a^{q}=\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}\!\int_{B_{r}(x_{o})}u^{q}(x,t_{o})\,\mathrm{d}x
is strictly positive, then
\nabla u^{1-q}
is locally bounded in a certain cylinder, whose center lies at time
t_{o}+a^{q-1}r^{2}
.

In the fast diffusion regime, (
p=2
&
q>1
) the picture is clearer. In the super-critical range
1<q<\frac{N}{(N-2)_{+}}
DiBenedetto & Kwong & Vespri
[
21
]
proved that weak solutions are locally analytic in the space variable and at least Lipschitz-continuous in time up to the extinction time. They presented very precise, quantitative, regularity estimates up to the boundary.

The first investigations of the gradient regularity of solutions to the doubly non-linear equation (
6.1
) are due to Ivanov & Mkrtychyan
[
31
]
, Ivanov
[
29
]
, and Savaré & Vespri
[
39
]
.
The results of
[
31
]
are not easily comparable with ours, since they are given for so-called
regular
solutions, that is, weak solutions to a Cauchy-Dirichlet problem built as suitable limits.
In the slow diffusion regime (
p>1
&
q<p-1
) which we do not deal with here, a bound for
\nabla u^{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(0,1)
holds for
q\leq\frac{p-1}{p}
.

As far as
\nabla u
is concerned, combining
[
29
, Theorem 6.1]
and the correction pointed out in
[
30
, § 5]
, yields the claim that the range of values of
p
and
q
in order for
\nabla u
to be bounded is

p>1,\quad 0<q\leq 1,\quad\frac{(p-1)(1-q)}{q}<\frac{2\min\{1,p-1\}}{2\min\{1,p-1\}+\frac{N}{4}}.

Even if we limit ourselves to the fast diffusion regime (
0<p-1<q
), which is the case we are interested in here, this does not correspond to the results of Theorem
6.1
, and it is not clear how the Cauchy-Dirichlet problem Ivanov starts from, affects the interior regularity of the gradient.

Finally,
in
[
39
, Theorem 1.5]
, with a different notion of solution to (
6.1
) Savaré & Vespri provided a gradient estimate

|\nabla u(x_{o},t_{o})|\leq\frac{Cu(x_{o},t_{o})}{\varrho}

for some
C=C(p,q,N)
and for parameters
p<N
,
0<p-1<q<\frac{N(p-1)}{N-p}
.

6.5.
Optimality

Let us now present some counterexamples which illustrate that the statement of Theorem
6.1
breaks down in the cases
q=p-1
and
q=\frac{N(p-1)}{(N-p)_{+}}
.

We start with
q=p-1
: in this case the function

u(x,t)=Ct^{-\frac{N}{p(p-1)}}\exp\left(-\frac{p-1}{p}\left(\frac{|x|^{p}}{pt}\right)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\right)

is a solution to (
6.1
) in
\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
for arbitrary
C>0
. If we choose
t=1
and
\varrho=1
and pick a sequence of points
x_{n}
such that
|x_{n}|\to\infty
, it is easy to see that (
6.2
) cannot hold. This fact was first pointed out in
[
39
, Remark 1.6]
.

The estimate (
6.2
) does not hold in general when
q\geq\frac{N(p-1)}{(N-p)_{+}}
. Suppose the estimate were to hold for some
u_{o}>0
. Then letting
\varrho\to\infty
it would have implied
u
is constant in
\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}
. However, this contradicts the existence of non-constant solutions. Indeed, for parameters

N\geq 2,\quad N>p,\quad q=\frac{N(p-1)}{N-p},\quad b=\left(\frac{N}{q}\right)^{q},

the function

u(x,t)=\left(|x|^{\frac{N(q+1)}{q(N-1)}}+e^{bt}\right)^{-\frac{N-1}{q+1}}

is a non-negative solution to (
6.1
) in
\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Moreover, the right-hand side of (
6.2
) reduces to
\frac{C}{\varrho}
. As for the left-hand side, considering the previous explicit solution,
we have

|\nabla u(x,t)|=\frac{N}{q}\frac{|x|^{\frac{N+q}{q(N-1)}}}{\left[|x|^{\frac{N(q+1)}{q(N-1)}}+e^{bt}\right]^{\frac{N+q}{q+1}}}.

It is apparent that

\sup_{Q_{o}}|\nabla u|\geq\sup_{K_{\varrho}}|\nabla u(\cdot,0)|=\sup_{r\in(0,\varrho)}f(r),

where we have set

|x|=r,\quad f(r)=\frac{N}{q}\frac{r^{\frac{N+q}{q(N-1)}}}{\left[r^{\frac{N(q+1)}{q(N-1)}}+1\right]^{\frac{N+q}{q+1}}}.

It is a matter of straightforward computations to check that if

\varrho>\left(\frac{1}{N-1}\right)^{\frac{q(N-1)}{N(q+1)}}:=\bar{r},

we have

\sup_{0<r<\varrho}f(r)=f(\bar{r})=C(N,q).

Hence,
\sup_{Q_{o}}|\nabla u|\geq C(N,q)
, and provided
\varrho
is chosen sufficiently large, (
6.2
) cannot hold.

6.6.
Proof of Theorem
6.1
and Corollary
6.3

The first step is given by the following result. It shows that once two-sided bounds are imposed to a solution, then it behaves like the one to the parabolic
p
-Laplace equation. Here,
p>1
and
q>0
can be arbitrary.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
p>1
,
q>0
, and
k>1
.
There are constants
C>1
and
\alpha_{o}\in(0,1)
, which depend only on
N,p,q
and
k
, such that if
u
is a
non-negative weak solution to (
6.1
) in
E_{T}
, and if

(6.9)

\tfrac{1}{k}\leq u\leq k\qquad\mbox{in $E_{T}$}

holds, then for any cylinder
Q_{\varrho}(z_{o})\subset E_{T}
we have the gradient bound

(6.10)

\sup_{Q_{\varrho/2}(z_{o})}|\nabla u|\leq\frac{C}{\varrho},

the Lipschitz estimate

(6.11)

|u(z_{1})-u(z_{2})|\leq C\Bigg[\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{\varrho^{p}}}\,\Bigg],

and the gradient Hölder estimate

(6.12)

|\nabla u(z_{1})-\nabla u(z_{2})|\leq\frac{C}{\varrho}\,\Bigg[\,\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{\varrho^{p}}}\,\Bigg]^{\alpha_{o}},

for all
z_{1}=(x_{1},t_{1}),z_{2}=(x_{2},t_{2})\in Q_{\varrho/8}(z_{o})
.

Proof..

We consider a cylinder
Q_{\varrho}(z_{o}):=K_{\varrho}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-\varrho^{p},t_{o}]\subset E_{T}
. Re-scaling to the unit cylinder
Q_{1}:=K_{1}(0)\times(-1,0]
leads to a weak solution

\displaystyle\tilde{u}(y,s):=u\big(x_{o}+\varrho y,t_{o}+\varrho^{p}s\big)\quad\mbox{for $(y,s)\in Q_{1}$,}

to the doubly non-linear parabolic equation

(6.13)

\partial_{t}\tilde{u}^{q}-\Div\big(|\nabla\tilde{u}|^{p-2}\nabla\tilde{u}\big)=0\quad\mbox{in $Q_{1}$,}

with values in
[k^{-1},k]
.
Substituting
v=\tilde{u}^{q}
, equation (
6.13
) is equivalent to

\partial_{t}v-\Div\Big(\big(\tfrac{1}{q}\big)^{p-1}\tilde{u}^{(p-1)(1-q)}|\nabla v|^{p-2}\nabla v\Big)=0\qquad\mbox{in $Q_{1}$}

and assumption (
6.9
) yields

(6.14)

\displaystyle k^{-q}\leq v\leq k^{q}\quad\mbox{in $Q_{1}$.}

With the abbreviation

\displaystyle a(y,s):=\big(\tfrac{1}{q}\big)^{p-1}[\tilde{u}(y,s)]^{(p-1)(1-q)},

the equation for
v
can be interpreted as a parabolic
p
-Laplace-type equation with
measurable coefficients
a(y,s)
in
Q_{1}
. Indeed,
v
is a bounded weak solution to

(6.15)

\partial_{t}v-\Div\big(a(y,s)|\nabla v|^{p-2}\nabla v\big)=0\qquad\mbox{in $Q_{1}$.}

For the coefficients
a
we have lower and upper bounds in terms of
k
, i.e.

(6.16)

\big(\tfrac{1}{q}\big)^{p-1}k^{-(p-1)|1-q|}\leq a(y,s)\leq\big(\tfrac{1}{q}\big)^{p-1}k^{(p-1)|1-q|}\quad\mbox{for a.e.~$(y,s)\in Q_{1}$.}

Therefore, we may apply Chapter III, § 1, Theorem 1.1, resp. Chapter IV, § 1, Theorem 1.1 in
[
17
]
to deduce that
v
is locally Hölder continuous in
Q_{1}
with some Hölder exponent
\alpha\in(0,1)
depending only on
N,p,q
and
k
.
In particular,
v
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous in
Q_{\frac{1}{2}}=K_{\frac{1}{2}}\times(-\frac{1}{4},0]
.
Recalling (
6.14
), the quantitative Hölder estimates from
[
17
]
yield

\displaystyle|v(y_{1},s)-v(y_{2},s)|\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{\alpha}

for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in K_{\frac{1}{2}}
and
s\in(-\frac{1}{4},0]
, where
C=C(N,p,q,k)
.
This and the lower and upper bound for
v
in (
6.14
) imply that
a
is also
\alpha
-Hölder continuous with the quantitative estimate

\displaystyle|a(y_{1},s)-a(y_{2},s)|\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{\alpha}

for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in K_{\frac{1}{2}}
and
s\in(-\frac{1}{4},0]
, where
C=C(N,p,q,k)
.
Together with (
6.15
) and (
6.16
) this shows that the hypotheses (
1.2
) of Theorem
1.1
(with
\mu=0
)
are fulfilled with constants
C_{o},C_{1}
depending only on
N,p,q,k
.
Therefore, the Schauder-estimates from the same theorem are at our disposal. More precisely, we have
\nabla v\in C^{\alpha_{o},\alpha_{o}/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(Q_{1/2},\mathbb{R}^{N})
for some Hölder exponent
\alpha_{o}\in(0,1)
, depending only on
N,p,q
and
k
.
Furthermore, the Schauder-estimates from the same theorem provide quantitative estimates. To be more precise, there exists a constant
C=C(N,p,q,k)\geq 1
such that for any compact subset
\mathcal{K}\subset Q_{1/2}
with

\varrho:=\tfrac{1}{4}\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{par}}(\mathcal{K},\partial_{\mathrm{par}}Q_{1/2})>0
,
the
L^{\infty}
-gradient bound

\sup_{\mathcal{K}}|\nabla v|\leq C\bigg[\frac{\bm{\omega}}{\varrho}+\Big(\frac{\bm{\omega}}{\varrho}\Big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\bigg]=:C\lambda,

and the
\alpha_{o}
-Hölder gradient estimate

|\nabla v(\mathfrak{z}_{1})-\nabla v(\mathfrak{z}_{2})|\leq C\lambda\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}^{(\lambda)}(\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2})}{\min\{1,\lambda^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\}\varrho}\bigg]^{\alpha_{o}}\quad\mbox{for any $\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2}\in\mathcal{K}$,}

hold true. Here we defined
\bm{\omega}:=\operatornamewithlimits{osc}_{Q_{\frac{1}{2}}}v
.
For
\mathcal{K}=Q_{\frac{1}{4}}
,
\varrho
can be precisely quantified,
and
\bm{\omega}\leq k^{q}
. Therefore, we have

\lambda=\frac{\bm{\omega}}{\varrho}+\Big(\frac{\bm{\omega}}{\varrho}\Big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\leq C(N,p,q,k),

so that

(6.17)

\sup_{Q_{1/4}}|\nabla v|\leq C(N,p,q,k),

and

(6.18)

|\nabla v(\mathfrak{z}_{1})-\nabla v(\mathfrak{z}_{2})|\leq C(N,p,q,k)\,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}(\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2})^{\alpha_{o}}\quad\mbox{for any $\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2}\in Q_{1/4}$.}

In the case
p>2
, to convert the intrinsic parabolic distance
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}^{(\lambda)}

into the parabolic distance
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}
, we take advantage of the fact that we may assume
\alpha_{o}\leq\frac{2}{p-2}
.

Once boundedness of
\nabla v
is established, we can derive a bound for the oscillation of
v
on
Q_{\frac{1}{8}}
by an application of Lemma
4.10
. Indeed, for two points
\mathfrak{z}_{1}=(y_{1},s_{1}),\,\mathfrak{z}_{2}=(y_{2},s_{2})\in Q_{\frac{1}{8}}
with
s_{1}\leq s_{2}
we apply Lemma
4.10
(with
\mu=0
) to
v
on the cylinder
Q=K_{r}(\tilde{y})\times(s_{1},s_{2}]\subset Q_{1/4}
, where
\tilde{y}=\frac{1}{2}(y_{1}+y_{2})
and
\frac{1}{2}|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq r\leq\frac{1}{8}
. The application yields

|v(\mathfrak{z}_{1})-v(\mathfrak{z}_{2})|\leq\operatornamewithlimits{osc}_{Q}v\leq C\bigg[r\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}+\frac{s_{2}-s_{1}}{r}\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}^{p-1}\bigg]\leq C\bigg[r+\frac{s_{2}-s_{1}}{r}\bigg].

Now, if
\sqrt{s_{2}-s_{1}}\leq|y_{1}-y_{2}|
we choose
r=\frac{1}{2}|y_{1}-y_{2}|
, so that the right-hand side is bounded by
C|y_{1}-y_{2}|
. Otherwise, if
\sqrt{s_{2}-s_{1}}>|y_{2}-y_{1}|
we choose
r=\sqrt{s_{2}-s_{1}}\leq\frac{1}{8}
which results in the bound
C\sqrt{s_{2}-s_{1}}
. Joining both cases, we obtain

|v(\mathfrak{z}_{1})-v(\mathfrak{z}_{2})|\leq C\,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}(\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2})\quad\mbox{for any $\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2}\in Q_{1/8}$}

for a constant
C=C(N,p,q,k)
.
In view of (
6.14
) this implies for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}

that

(6.19)

|v^{\beta}(\mathfrak{z}_{1})-v^{\beta}(\mathfrak{z}_{2})|\leq C\,k^{q|\beta-1|}\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{par}}(\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2})\quad\mbox{for any $\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2}\in Q_{1/8}$, }

where
C=C(N,p,q,\beta,k)
.

Finally, we scale back to the original solution
u
. From (
6.19
) with
\beta=\frac{1}{q}
we obtain

|u(z_{1})-u(z_{2})|\leq C\Bigg[\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{\varrho^{p}}}\,\Bigg]\quad\mbox{for any $z_{1},z_{2}\in Q_{\varrho/8}$,}

where
C=C(N,p,q,k)
.
This proves (
6.11
).
Next, computing the spatial gradient
\nabla u
in terms of
v
gives

\displaystyle\nabla u(x,t)=\frac{v^{\frac{1}{q}-1}}{q\varrho}\bigg(\frac{x-x_{o}}{\varrho}\,,\,\frac{t-t_{o}}{\varrho^{p}}\bigg)\nabla v\bigg(\frac{x-x_{o}}{\varrho}\,,\,\frac{t-t_{o}}{\varrho^{p}}\bigg).

Then, (
6.14
) and (
6.17
) imply

\sup_{Q_{\varrho/4}}|\nabla u|\leq\frac{C}{\varrho}.

By the same modifications as before, this yields (
6.10
).
Combining (
6.17
), (
6.18
), and (
6.19
) (with
\beta=\frac{1}{q}-1
) gives

|\nabla u(z_{1})-\nabla u(z_{2})|\leq\frac{C}{\varrho}\Bigg[\,\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{\varrho^{p}}}\,\Bigg]^{\alpha_{o}}

for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in Q_{\varrho/8}
, which implies (
6.12
) as before. ∎

Remark 6.9
.

Note that Theorem
6.8
continues to hold for symmetric cylinders of the type
\mathcal{Q}_{\varrho}:=K_{\varrho}(0)\times(-\varrho^{p},\varrho^{p})
.
In fact, in the statement and the proof, the one-sided cylinders
Q_{\varrho}(z_{o})
can be replaced by the symmetric ones
\mathcal{Q}_{\varrho}
.

We are now in the position to prove the main regularity results for doubly non-linear equations. Essentially, in the super-critical regime the artificial condition (
6.9
) can be quantified by Harnack’s inequality, and hence regularity estimates are formulated with respect to the equation’s own intrinsic geometry.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

As before, we note that the continuity of
u
is only used in order to give
u(x_{o},t_{o})
an unambiguous meaning; see also Remark
6.2
.
From
[
8
, Theorem 1.5]

we infer that
u
is locally bounded in
E_{T}
.
We consider
z_{o}=(x_{o},t_{o})\in E_{T}
such that
u_{o}:=u(x_{o},t_{o})>0
. By
\sigma=\sigma(N,p,q)\in(0,1)
and

\bm{\gamma}_{\mathrm{h}}=\bm{\gamma}_{\mathrm{h}}(N,p,q)>1
we denote the constants from the Harnack inequality of
[
8
, Theorem 1.11]
.
Now, let
\varrho>0
and define
\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}:=\frac{8{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{h}}}{\sigma}>1
and assume that

K_{\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho}(x_{o})\times\big(t_{o}-u_{o}^{q+1-p}(\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho)^{p},t_{o}+u_{o}^{q+1-p}(\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho)^{p}\big)\subset E_{T}.

Then the smaller cylinder

K_{8\varrho/\sigma^{\frac{1}{p}}}(x_{o})\times\Big(t_{o}-{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{h}}u_{o}^{q+1-p}\big(8\varrho/\sigma^{\frac{1}{p}}\big)^{p},t_{o}+{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{h}}u_{o}^{q+1-p}\big(8\varrho/\sigma^{\frac{1}{p}}\big)^{p}\Big)

is also contained in
E_{T}
, so that we are allowed to apply Harnack’s inequality on this cylinder. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle Q_{o}

\displaystyle:=K_{\varrho}(x_{o})\times\big(t_{o}-u_{o}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p},t_{o}+u_{o}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}\big)

\displaystyle\,\subset K_{\varrho/\sigma^{\frac{1}{p}}}(x_{o})\times\big(t_{o}-u_{o}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p},t_{o}+u_{o}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}\big)

\displaystyle\,=K_{\varrho/\sigma^{\frac{1}{p}}}(x_{o})\times\Big(t_{o}-\sigma u_{o}^{q+1-p}\big(\varrho/\sigma^{\frac{1}{p}}\big)^{p},t_{o}+\sigma u_{o}^{q+1-p}\big(\varrho/\sigma^{\frac{1}{p}}\big)^{p}\Big).

Therefore, by Harnack’s inequality we conclude

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\bm{\gamma}_{\mathrm{h}}}u_{o}\leq u(x,t)\leq\bm{\gamma}_{\mathrm{h}}u_{o}\quad\mbox{for a.e.~$(x,t)\in Q_{o}$.}

Re-scaling to the symmetric cylinder
\mathcal{Q}_{\varrho}:=K_{\varrho}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-\varrho^{p},t_{o}+\varrho^{p})
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\tilde{u}(y,s):=\tfrac{1}{u_{o}}\,u\big(y,u_{o}^{q+1-p}s\big)\quad\mbox{for $(y,s)\in\mathcal{Q}_{\varrho}(z_{o})$,}

leads to a weak solution
\tilde{u}
to the doubly non-linear parabolic equation

\partial_{t}\tilde{u}^{q}-\Div\big(|\nabla\tilde{u}|^{p-2}\nabla\tilde{u}\big)=0\quad\mbox{in $\mathcal{Q}_{\varrho}(z_{o})$,}

with values in
[{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{h}}^{-1},{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{h}}]
. At this point we can apply Theorem
6.8
to
\tilde{u}
in
\mathcal{Q}_{\varrho}(z_{o})
(recall Remark
6.9
), to infer that

\sup_{\mathcal{Q}_{\varrho/2}(z_{o})}|\nabla\tilde{u}|\leq\frac{C}{\varrho},

the Lipschitz estimate

|\tilde{u}(z_{1})-\tilde{u}(z_{2})|\leq C\Bigg[\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{\varrho^{p}}}\,\Bigg],

and the gradient Hölder estimate

|\nabla\tilde{u}(z_{1})-\nabla\tilde{u}(z_{2})|\leq\frac{C}{\varrho}\,\Bigg[\,\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{\varrho^{p}}}\,\Bigg]^{\alpha_{o}},

for all
z_{1}=(x_{1},t_{1}),z_{2}=(x_{2},t_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\varrho/8}(z_{o})
.
Rescaling back to the original solution
u
yields the assertions of Theorem
6.1
.
∎

An immediate consequence of Theorem
6.1
is as follows.

Corollary 6.10
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.1
we have

|u(z_{1})-u(z_{2})|\leq\frac{Cru_{o}}{\varrho}.

and

|\nabla u(z_{1})-\nabla u(z_{2})|\leq C\Big(\frac{r}{\varrho}\Big)^{\alpha_{o}}\,\frac{u_{o}}{\varrho}

for any points
z_{1},z_{2}\in K_{r}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-u_{o}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p-2}r^{2},t_{o}+u_{o}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p-2}r^{2})
and any radius
0<r\leq\varrho
.

Proof..

The direct application of (
6.3
), respectively (
6.4
) yields the claimed estimates.
∎

Next, we show how to extend the gradient-sup-bound and the
C^{1,\alpha}
-estimates to general compact subsets in
E_{T}
. This is exactly the assertion of Corollary
6.3
.

Proof of Corollary
6.3
.

Let
\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}>1
be the constant from Theorem
6.1
and
\varrho:=\tfrac{1}{\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}}\mathcal{M}^{-\frac{q+1-p}{p}}\varrho_{o}
. Then the definition of
\varrho_{o}
implies

K_{\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho}(x)\times\big(t-\mathcal{M}^{q+1-p}(\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho)^{p},t+\mathcal{M}^{q+1-p}(\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho)^{p}\big)\Subset E_{T}\qquad\mbox{for all }(x,t)\in\mathcal{K}.

Let us fix two points
(x_{i},t_{i})\in\mathcal{K}
and denote
u_{i}:=u(x_{i},t_{i})
for
i=1,2
.
If
u_{i}>0
, we can apply the gradient bound (
6.2
) on the cylinders

Q_{i}:=K_{\varrho}(x_{i})\times\big(t_{i}-u_{i}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p},t_{i}+u_{i}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}\big)\Subset E_{T},

which implies in particular

(6.20)

\displaystyle|\nabla u(x_{i},t_{i})|\leq C\frac{u_{i}}{\varrho}\leq C\frac{\mathcal{M}}{\varrho}=C\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\frac{q+1}{p}}}{\varrho_{o}}.

In the case
u_{i}=0
, however, we have
u=0

on the whole time slice
E\times\{t_{i}\}
, since otherwise,
repeated applications of Harnack’s inequality would imply
u_{i}>0
.
Therefore, we infer
\nabla u(x_{i},t_{i})=0
, so that (
6.20
) is trivially satisfied also in this case.
Since
(x_{i},t_{i})\in\mathcal{K}
is arbitrary, this implies the asserted gradient bound (
6.5
).

For the proof of the
C^{1,\alpha}
-estimate, we distinguish two cases.

Case 1:

|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\varrho
and
|t_{1}-t_{2}|<\max\{u_{1},u_{2}\}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
u_{1}\geq u_{2}
, so that

|t_{1}-t_{2}|<u_{1}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}
. Note that this implies in particular
u_{1}>0
. The case
u_{1}=u_{2}=0
is included in Case 2. In the present situation we have

(6.21)

(x_{2},t_{2})\in Q_{1}:=K_{\varrho}(x_{1})\times\big(t_{1}-u_{1}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p},t_{1}+u_{1}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}\big).

Therefore, the
C^{1,\alpha}
-estimate (
6.4
) on the cylinder
Q_{1}
implies

|\nabla u(x_{1},t_{1})-\nabla u(x_{2},t_{2})|\leq C\frac{u_{1}}{\varrho}\Bigg[\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{u_{1}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}}}\,\Bigg]^{\alpha_{o}}.

In view of (
6.21
), the term in brackets on the
right-hand side is bounded by
2
. Therefore, we may diminish the
power
\alpha_{o}
and replace it by

\alpha_{1}:=\min\{\alpha_{o},\frac{2}{q+1-p}\}
. The resulting term is
increasing in
u_{1}
, so that we can bound it from above by replacing

u_{1}
by
\mathcal{M}
. Hence, we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle|\nabla u(x_{1},t_{1})-\nabla u(x_{2},t_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\mathcal{M}}{\varrho}\Bigg[\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{\mathcal{M}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}}}\,\Bigg]^{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\frac{q+1}{p}}}{\varrho_{o}}\Bigg[\mathcal{M}^{\frac{q+1-p}{p}}\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho_{o}}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{\varrho_{o}^{p}}}\,\Bigg]^{\alpha_{1}}.

In the last step, we used the definition of
\varrho
. This yields the
asserted estimate (
6.6
) in the first case.

Case 2:

|x_{1}-x_{2}|\geq\varrho
or

|t_{1}-t_{2}|\geq\max\{u_{1},u_{2}\}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}
.

In this case, we use the gradient bound (
6.20
), which implies

|\nabla u(x_{i},t_{i})|\leq C\frac{u_{i}}{\varrho}\leq C\frac{u_{i}}{\varrho}\Bigg[\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{u_{i}^{q+1-p}\varrho^{p}}}\,\Bigg]^{\alpha_{1}},

since the term in the brackets is bounded from below by one in the
current case.
Similarly as above, we can
further estimate the right-hand side by monotonicity and replace
u_{i}
by
\mathcal{M}
. In this way, we again obtain

\displaystyle|\nabla u(x_{1},t_{1})-\nabla u(x_{2},t_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla u(x_{1},t_{1})|+|\nabla u(x_{2},t_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\frac{q+1}{p}}}{\varrho_{o}}\Bigg[\mathcal{M}^{\frac{q+1-p}{p}}\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\varrho_{o}}+\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{\varrho_{o}^{p}}}\,\Bigg]^{\alpha_{1}}.

This establishes the claim (
6.6
) in the second case as well.
∎

6.7.
Decay estimates at the extinction time

In the fast diffusion range solutions might become extinct abruptly. This means that for some
T>0
we have
u(\cdot,T)=0
a.e. in
E
. Such a time
T
is called
extinction time
. In this section we prove the decay estimates at the extinction time stated in Corollary
6.7
. Subsequently we derive an estimate for the extinction time of the solution to a Cauchy-Dirichlet problem in terms of the measure of the set
E
and the
L^{q+1}
-norm of the initial values
u_{o}
.

Proof of Corollary
6.7
.

For
x_{o}\in E
and
t_{o}\in(\tfrac{1}{2}T,T)
, we apply the integral Harnack inequality from
[
8
, Theorem 1.13]
on the
cylinder
K_{d(x_{o})}(x_{o})\times(T-2(T-t_{o}),T]\subset E_{T}
. Since

u(\cdot,T)=0
, we infer the bound

u_{o}:=u(x_{o},t_{o})\leq C\bigg[\frac{T-t_{o}}{d^{p}(x_{o})}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{q+1-p}}

with a constant
C
depending on
N,p
, and
q
. This yields the first asserted estimate. Next, we choose
\varrho_{o}:={\bm{\gamma}}_{o}^{-1}d(x_{o})
, where the
constant
{\bm{\gamma}}_{o}>\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}
is at our disposal. From the last displayed estimate we have

u_{o}^{q+1-p}(\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho_{o})^{p}\leq C^{q+1-p}\frac{T-t_{o}}{d^{p}(x_{o})}\bigg[\frac{\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}d(x_{o})}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{o}}\bigg]^{p}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(T-t_{o}).

The last inequality follows by choosing
{\bm{\gamma}}_{o}
sufficiently large.
The above choice of
\varrho_{o}
therefore implies

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}Q_{o}

\displaystyle:=K_{\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho_{o}}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-u_{o}^{q+1-p}(\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho_{o})^{p},t_{o}+u_{o}^{q+1-p}(\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho_{o})^{p})

\displaystyle\,\subset K_{\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}\varrho_{o}}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-\tfrac{1}{2}(T-t_{o}),t_{o}+\tfrac{1}{2}(T-t_{o}))\Subset E_{T}.

Therefore, the gradient estimate (
6.2
) is available on the cylinder

Q_{o}
. By combining this estimate with the already established bound for
u
, we infer

|\nabla u(x_{o},t_{o})|\leq\frac{Cu_{o}}{\varrho_{o}}\leq\frac{C}{d(x_{o})}\bigg[\frac{T-t_{o}}{d^{p}(x_{o})}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{q+1-p}}.

Moreover, for
r\in(0,\varrho_{o})
we obtain from (
6.3
) that

\operatornamewithlimits{osc}_{K_{r}(x_{o})}u(\cdot,t_{o})\leq\frac{Cru_{o}}{\varrho_{o}}\leq C\frac{r}{d(x_{o})}\bigg[\frac{T-t_{o}}{d^{p}(x_{o})}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{q+1-p}}

and from (
6.4
) that

\displaystyle\operatornamewithlimits{osc}_{K_{r}(x_{o})}\nabla u(\cdot,t_{o})\leq C\Big(\frac{r}{\varrho_{o}}\Big)^{\alpha_{o}}\,\frac{u_{o}}{\varrho_{o}}\leq\frac{C}{d(x_{o})}\bigg[\frac{r}{d(x_{o})}\bigg]^{\alpha_{o}}\bigg[\frac{T-t_{o}}{d^{p}(x_{o})}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{q+1-p}},

which completes the proof.
∎

Concerning the extinction time
T
we refer to Corollary
6.7
, where an upper bound for it can be given in a simple but nevertheless interesting situation. Consider the Cauchy-Dirichlet Problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}\big(|u|^{q-1}u\big)-\Div(|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u)=0&\quad\mbox{in $E_{T}$,}\\[6.0pt]
u=0&\quad\mbox{on $\partial E\times(0,T]$,}\\[6.0pt]
u(\cdot,0)=u_{o}&\quad\mbox{in $E$,}\end{array}\right.

where
T\in(0,\infty]
and the initial datum
u_{o}\geq 0
is non-negative. For finite
T<\infty
the existence of a non-negative solution to the Cauchy-Dirichlet Problem is discussed in
[
8
, Chapter 4, Remark 4.5]
. The case
T=\infty
follows by the uniqueness from
[
8
, Chapter 4, Corollary 4.7]
.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
0<p-1<q<\frac{N(p-1)+p}{(N-p)_{+}}
,

E\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
a bounded domain, and
u_{o}\in L^{q+1}(E)
with
u_{o}\geq 0
. Moreover, let

u\in C\big([0,+\infty);L^{q+1}(E)\big)\cap L^{p}\big(0,+\infty;W^{1,p}_{0}(E)\big)

be the unique non-negative weak solution to the Cauchy-Dirichlet problem. There exists a finite time
T>0
depending only on
N
,
p
,
q
,
|E|
, and
u_{o}
, such that

u(\cdot,t)=0\quad\text{ for all }\,\,t\geq T.

Moreover, we have

0<T\leq\frac{q\,C^{p}}{q+1-p}\,|E|^{\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{N(q+1)}}\|u_{o}\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1-p},

where
C
is a constant
that depends on
N
and
p
,
and
\lambda_{q+1}:=N(p-q-1)+p(q+1)
.

Proof..

Taking
u
as test function in the weak formulation of the Cauchy-Dirichlet Problem,
[
2
, Lemma 1.5]
yields

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1}-\|u_{o}\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1}+\frac{q+1}{q}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{p}(E)}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\tau=0

for any
t>0
. Let us first assume that
1<p<N
; the ranges of
p
and
q
ensure that
q+1<\frac{Np}{N-p}=:p_{\ast}
, so that the Hölder and the Sobolev inequalities give

(6.22)

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}\leq|E|^{\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{Np(q+1)}}\,\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p_{*}}(E)}\leq C|E|^{\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{Np(q+1)}}\,\|\nabla u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(E)},

where
C=C(N,p)
is the optimal constant of the Sobolev inequality.

Next, we consider
the case
p\geq N\geq 1
.
Let us first suppose
N\geq 2
and take
s\in(1,N)
that satisfies
s_{*}:=\frac{Ns}{N-s}>q+1
; by Hölder’s inequality, we have

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}\leq|E|^{\frac{1}{q+1}-\frac{N-s}{Ns}}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s_{*}}(E)}.

By the Sobolev and Hölder inequalities, we estimate

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s_{*}}(E)}\leq C\|\nabla u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(E)}\leq C|E|^{\frac{1}{s}-\frac{1}{p}}\|\nabla u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(E)},

where again
C=C(N,s)
is the optimal constant of the Sobolev inequality.
Joining the last two inequalities yields inequality (
6.22
)
in the case
p\geq N\geq 2
. If
N=1
, by the Sobolev inequality we directly obtain that

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}\leq|E|^{\frac{1}{q+1}}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(E)}\leq C|E|^{\frac{1}{q+1}+1-\frac{1}{p}}\|\nabla u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(E)},

and once more we end up with (
6.22
).

Consequently, combining the previous estimates yields in any case that

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1}-\|u_{o}\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1}+\frac{q+1}{qC^{p}|E|^{\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{N(q+1)}}}\int_{0}^{t}\|u(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\leq 0

for every
t>0
.
Setting
\displaystyle v(t):=\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1}
, the previous inequality can be rewritten as

v(t)-v(0)+\mu\int_{0}^{t}[v(\tau)]^{\frac{p}{q+1}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\leq 0,

where
v(0)=\|u_{o}\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1}
and

\mu:=\frac{q+1}{qC^{p}|E|^{\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{N(q+1)}}}.

By the regularity of
u
,
v
is a continuous function of
t
.
Just as before, we can prove that

v(t_{2})-v(t_{1})\leq-\mu\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}[v(\tau)]^{\frac{p}{q+1}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\qquad\forall\,t_{2}>t_{1}\geq 0.

Since
v\geq 0
by definition, it is apparent that
v
is strictly decreasing wherever it is positive,
\lim_{t\to\infty}v(t)=0
, and if there exists
T>0
such that
v(T)=0
, then
v(t)=0
for any
t\geq T
.

Now, let
w:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be the unique solution to the Cauchy Problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}w^{\prime}(t)=-\mu[w(t)]^{\frac{p}{q+1}},\\[6.0pt]
w(0)=v(0),\end{array}\right.

which can be computed as

w(t)=v(0)\left[1-\frac{\mu(q+1-p)}{(q+1)[v(0)]^{\frac{q+1-p}{q+1}}}\,t\right]_{+}^{\frac{q+1}{q+1-p}}.

We obviously have

w(t_{2})-w(t_{1})=-\mu\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}[w(\tau)]^{\frac{p}{q+1}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\qquad\forall\,t_{2}>t_{1}\geq 0.

Suppose there exists
\tilde{t}\in(0,\infty)
such that
v(\tilde{t})>w(\tilde{t})
and let

t_{o}:=\sup\big\{t\in(0,\tilde{t}):\,v(t)\leq w(t)\big\}.

We have
v(t_{o})=w(t_{o})
, and for any
t\in[t_{o},\tilde{t}\,]
there holds

v(t)-v(t_{o})\leq-\mu\int_{t_{o}}^{t}[v(\tau)]^{\frac{p}{q+1}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau,\quad w(t)-v(t_{o})=-\mu\int_{t_{o}}^{t}[w(\tau)]^{\frac{p}{q+1}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Subtracting from one another yields that for any
t\in(t_{o},\tilde{t}]

v(t)-w(t)\leq-\mu\int_{t_{o}}^{t}\left([v(\tau)]^{\frac{p}{q+1}}-[w(\tau)]^{\frac{p}{q+1}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

which is a contradiction, since the left-hand side is positive, whereas the right-hand side is negative. Hence, we necessarily conclude that

v(t)\leq w(t)\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,\infty)

which gives

v(t)\leq v(0)\left[1-\frac{\mu(q+1-p)}{(q+1)[v(0)]^{\frac{q+1-p}{q+1}}}\,t\right]_{+}^{\frac{q+1}{q+1-p}}.

From this, reverting to
\|u(\cdot,t)||_{L^{q+1}(E)}
, we have

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}\leq\|u_{o}\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}\left[1-\frac{q+1-p}{qC^{p}|E|^{\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{N(q+1)}}\|u_{o}\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1-p}}\,t\right]_{+}^{\frac{1}{q+1-p}},

and

0<T\leq\frac{q\,C^{p}}{q+1-p}\,|E|^{\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{N(q+1)}}\|u_{o}\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1-p}.

This proves the claim.
∎

Remark 6.12
.

The same estimate of the extinction time
T
is given in
[
37
, Proposition 3.5]
, assuming
1<p<N
,
q\geq 1
,
p-1<q<\frac{N(p-1)+p}{N-p}
. In this paper there is also an estimate from below in the same ranges for
p
and
q
. Indeed, in
[
37
, Proposition 4.2 and Corollary 4.3]
Misawa & Nakamura & Sarkar prove that when
u_{o}\in W^{1,p}_{0}(E)
one has

T\geq\frac{q}{q+1-p}\frac{\|u_{o}\|_{L^{q+1}(E)}^{q+1}}{\|Du_{o}\|_{L^{p}(E)}^{p}}.
